COMPULSORY "EXILE."

EDUCATION COMMITTEE DECLINES TO SUPPORT EMIGRATION PROPOSALS.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
THE RIGHT OF THE PARENT.

What one speaker termed the compulsory exile of children who have passed through industrial schools and are unable to find suitable openings in the British labour market was under discussion at the meeting of the Warrington Education Committee on Monday evening, a recommendation having been received from the Wimbledon Education Committee that the powers of the Home Secretary with regard to emigration should be extended. Councillor H Roberts prEsided and there we-e also present :—Mr A J Jolley (deputy- chairman), Alderman A Bennett. Councillors T Birtles, J Wilkinson, W II J Jenkins, R Henshall, J Chrimes, Archer, J Pemberton, E Lindley, E Osborne and J Broadhurst, Mrs C Jackson Holmes, Revs Canon Morley Stevenson, 0 G Biiggs and Neville V Wilson, Messrs C E Parker, Gi Walker, E Plinston, J H Kiogrion, C S Burgess, R r Fairclough. C H Hnsworth, F Roberts, J Moore Murray (director) and It B Roberts (assistant director). TEACHERS' PLACE OF RESIDENCE.

At a meeting of the School Management Committee a draft circular letter to the Managers of the public elementary schools in the Borough upon the subject of the place of residence of teachers hereafter appointed and the length of service to be rendered by them was approved. The Rev Canon STEVENSON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that some little while ago, a pro- position was brought forward by the School Manage- ment Committee to the effect that the teachers' place of resider ce should be within a certain distance of the borough. That did not tind favour with the general committee and it was referred hack. the above recom- mendation being the outcome. It was to the effect that they should ask the managers of the schools, in making appointments, to have regard to the residence of the teacher—to see that it was in such a locality, not necessarily within a certain distance, but with such facilities for travel as would enable the teacher to get to school at a reasonable hour, without di fticulty.lt was also requested that managers would try to secure that a teacher should remain in the service of the authority a reasonable length of time and not throw up the appointment ins month or two and go elsetahere. That was extremely bad for the school and gave a great deal of trouble to the managers. TO ShE " HOW THEY SHAPE."

A communication was submitted from the Board of Education intimating, in connection with the decision of the committee at their meeting On the 15th February 1909 (a), that the Board have now decided that, where Local Education Authorities think it desirable to give intending public elementary school teachers who are hursari or candidates Vr bursarship a short period of teaching experience in a public elementary school either within or just before the bursar period, no objection to such a course would be rai-ett by the Board, an,i asking to be supplied with particulars as to the results of any such tebt that may be imposed by the committee in the future. Canon bTEVENSON said the committee had felt for a

lot;i4tltei-t-lx-t;t-th;:e was great risk in appointing young people as bursars or student teachers without ascertaining whether they had any capability for teaching. It was now placed within their power by the Board of Education to exact some simple test to see to use a Lancashire expression, " whether he shapes," before they went further in the matter. He thought that would commend itself to the good sense ot the committee and they congratulated the Director, who had carried on the negotiations, upon having obtained the consent ot the Board of Education to what certainly would be an interesting experiment. Mr UPSON seconded and the mintiti.s were approved. COMPULSORY "EXILE."

At a meeting of the School Attendance Committee a suggestion from the Wimbledon Education Goo- mittee with reference to the power conferred upon the Secretary of State by the Children Act, 1308, to authorise the emigration of certain children committed to indust rial schools was not agreed to. Mr PLINST,3N moved and Councillor BIRTEES seconded the adoption of the minutes. Alderman BENNETT asked for information as to the emigration proposals. Mr PLINSToN said there was sometimes a difficulty in obtaining suitable employment for children who had passed through industrial schools. They were at liberty to return home but in many cases the home was not fit for them to live in The Wimbledon Education Committee desired power to draft such children to situations abroad, even against the desire and wish of the parent, if necessary. At present they could not legally do that. The proposal was not agreed to by the School Attendance Committee and therefore the old conditions would remain, as far as they were concerned. Alderman BENNETT : It is not against the principle of emigration but against emigrating children without the consent of their parents, that the ;ommittee have decided ?

r PLINsToN : Qoite so. The CHAIRMAN : 1 take it that the object of the suggestion from the Wimbledon Education Committee was to give local education authorities power to place children out in suitable positions where, in their opinion, return to the homes of the parents would be decidedly to the disedvantage of the child? Mr PLINSTON : That is so. At present we cannot do it. The CHAIRMAN: At present, in some cases, parents wish to exploit the children as soon as they possibly min, for the purpose of adding to the home earnings, irrespective of any consideration for the interests of the child. Mr UPSON: Then why not agree to the proposal? Mr PLINSTON : The majority of the sub-committee was against it, and it behoves all sub-committees to obey the majority. The CHAtamAs : Perhaps a hint from the larger committee, in favour of the adoption of the recora- mendationo,night assist you. The Rev Father WiLsos said he was one who rather forcibly oppozed the proposal at the meeting of the sub-committee. The communication from the Wimble- don Education Committee, as tar as he understood it, advocated a very considerable extension of the present law. The youths who would be affected by the change were of the age of seventeen or eighteen and it was sought to give the Home Secretary absolutely the power of emigrating—or as he (the speaker) would call It, exiling them from their native land, without even ask- ing their own consent. Surely a young man of tbat age should have some say in the question of whether he was to leave his own country—probably for ever—or not. The proposal also ignored the authority of the parents, without any special reference as to whether they were likely to exercise it in a proper way. Tbe minutes were confirmed. ARCHITECTS FOR NEW SCHOOLS.

At a meeting of the Si-tee and Buildings Commit.ee a letter was reed from the architects for the Oakwood- avenue Council school in answer to the committee's enquiry in connection with the preparation by them of the plans and quantities for and the supervision of the erection of the two one-department schools to be pro- vided in Oliver-street and Parr-street in addition to the school in Oekwood-avenne. It was resolved that the architects for the Oakwood-avenue Council school be also appointed the architects for the schools to be erected in Oliver-street and Parr-street upon the terms set forth in their letter. Mr FAIRCLOUGH, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said the plans for the Oakwod-avenue Council school were thrown open to competition and a local firm of .architects was successful. The schools that

had previously been built to the designs of that firm bad never required any additional expenditure upon them since their erection and they were the best in the town. The committee carefully discussed the question and came to the conclusion that if they could get the same architects to take up the work in Oliver- street and Parr-street, they would be serving the best interests of the town. He did not think it was advisable to make the terms public, but there would be no objec- tion to any member of the committee being furnished with the information on applying to the Director. Mr WALKER seconded and the minutes were passed. THE CHILDREN'S TEETH.

A suggestion that an evening lecture to parents of school children upon the subject of tie care of the children's teeth should be given by the School Dental Officer in one of the schools was approved by the Medical Inspection Committee and authority was given for the necessary expense to be incurred in connection with the advertising of the lectu:e and its illustration by lantern slides. Consideration of a further sugges- tion that an effort should be made to induce and assist parents to obtain tooth brushes for use by their children was deferred for further information upon the subject.

KINGDON, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said they were making a new departure with regard to the dental clinio and they hoped it would meet with the approval of the committee. Mrs HoLmEs seconded.

Alderman BENNETT welcomed the proposal to give lectures to parents on the care of the children's teeth. He noticed, however, that up to the present only one lecture had been arranged, but he supposed it was simply an experiment.

Mr.liiNanoN replied in the affirmative. ABSENCE THROUGH SICKNESS.

Mr FAIRCLOUGIT asked why there were so many children absent throne h sickness at the present time. The absentees were o r 200 more than at the corres- ponding time last p . Mr KINGDOM said ,vould have to Plead the same cause as the insure!), I icy—an act of God. It was possible that the in • ion was a little more carefully done now than , ears gone by. Children used to attend school whci iy ought not to have done. The Medical Inspection i—nnmittee was simply carrying out the law of the land. Tb,l, had a certain standard fixed for them and, if a hid did not come 'up to that standard, be wag excluf d from school. The minutes were cotiirmed.

The Parish Church.

On Monday a special vestry meeting in connection with the Parish Cnurch was held in Fairfield schools to consider the new vestry scheme. The Rector presided, and among others present were the Revs A J Watt and 3 NI Buckmaster, Councillor Craik (Rector's warden), Mr Edward Isherwood (people's warden), MrEA White (vestry clerk) and Mr Geoffrey Owen I representing the architects, Wm. and Segar Owen). The plans of the proposed new vestty were produced by Mr Oxen and carefully inspected, after which the following resolution was passed, on the motion of Mr S Taylor, seconded by Mr Isherwood : " That the plans laid before the meeting for erecting a new vestry on he north side of the chancel of the Parish Church, for n eking certain alterations in the windows, for raking down edisused chimney and for slightly raising the ~.00th well of the existing vestry, be approved of, and that a Faculty for carrying out the same be applied fr." At the Liverpool Consistory Court en Wednesday, the faculty was granted.

English Church Union. " Protestantism No Place in the Church of Engind.- Protes'anl ism, ace( /doh., to the Rev R T Brockman, vicar of St John the Ilaptiat's, Tuebrook, Liverpool, has no place in the formularies of the Church of Engl.nd. The rev gentleman Wes the principal speaker at the annual meeting of the Warrington branch of the English Church Union held in the Masonic Hall, Bold- street, on Saturday afternoon and, in a forceful and outspoken way, he discoursed on "Tire conversion of England to the Catholic faith." . .

First came the election of officers of the branch, the following being chosen :—Chairmau, the Rev W J Stanton ; vice-caairman and treasurer, Mr C S Burgess; secretary, the Rev J H G Hollingworth. Ile Rev W J STINTON, aho presided, emphasised the necessity of combating the merely Frastian view of Church defence in the Welsh Church couiroversy. They held that the property of the Welsh Church had been given by Catholics in times past for the use of the Catholic Church, and they regarded it as the inheritance of the Welsh Church to-day, tecause they firmly believed that that Church was the Catholic Church of Wales. The mere incident of the Reformation made no differ- ence whatever—it was the same Church now as in tbo days of old, and the endowments given by pious people for the teaching of the people in

Wycliffe Congregational Chorelr.

The Rev S Archibald Parsons, M.A., of Darwen, preached at this church on Sunday. Speaking specially to the children at the morning service he advised them to keep a smiling face and cheerful countenance. The late Dr Barnardo realised the value of this in his rescue work, and all the boys admitted to his homes wore a button bearing the magic letters K.S (keep smiling) and all the girls a brooch with the same motto. The preacher pointed out that this watchword was essentially Christ-like. The sermon was based on Paul's words "I must also see Rome "(acts xix., 21). It was the dream, the preacher said, of Paul's life to see Rome, then the centre of the world. The great apostle con- ceived the magnificent ides of bringing a knowledge of Christ to the Roman citizens so that they might carry the gospel to the ends of the I earth. This dreaming of Paul's was typical of man's experience. In almost every life there was some great hope, some hidden desire. For the most part our dreams remained dreams. To Paul the dream of his life was realised, but id a sense it was his life's dis- appointment. To many of us the non-fulfilment of our dreams was our disappointment, but it was better to I have dreamt and never realised than never to have dreamt at all. Some people were always seeking a life of ease and pleasure, and others that of mere money- making, but these would never give peace. The evening discourse was taken from the book of Esther and was =slaty a study of the character of Haman. Ile pointed out that the book did not mention God, but it extolled virtue and moral courage and devotion and was therefore part of the word of God. The preacher commended the moral courage of Mordecai and drew a lesson from the vanity of Haman with its terrible nemesis. Vanity, he said, was a subtle deceiver and destroyed everyone who embraced her. He eondemn.d self-pity, self-conceit and self-gratification. All sin was selfishness, and what was selfishness but vanity? Next Sunday, the Rev T T James, M.A , of Wycliffe Chapel, Sheffield, will occupy the pulpit.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
the Catholic faith were hers by right. Touching upon welcome sigrs of a desire for Christian re-union, the Chairman said they as members of the Church of England were limited as to the way in which they regarded that question by their doctrine as to Holy Orders. Some deplorable things had been said and done by those in anthority which seemed to make light of the Catholic position of the Church of England and almost to regard it as one of a multitude of Protestant sects. The question of re-union in England lesulved itself largely into a question of Holy Orders and of Apostolieal succession. They believed that Christ gave His commission to His Apostles and that, through them, the grace of Holy Orders had been handed down to the present time. They could not possibly regara those ministers of other denominations who bad not received the laying on of bishop's hands as the same as the priests of the Catholic Church. They might honour them highly, they might esteem their work and see in them a deep spirituality ; they might deplore, perhaps, that they of the Church I of England did not make the same use of their far greater opportunities. But none of those things proved that a Dissenting minister was the same as a priest of the Church of England. It only proved, alas, that the members of the Church of England did not always make as good Use of their far higher grace and privileges as the members of the Dissenting corn- Munions did of theirs. Referring to the war in the Balkans, the chairman said their prayers must go out that God would restore to the Cross those lands in which the Crescent had usurped the rulership, that in His own good time He would show forth his truth to those who for so many centuries had proved obdurate and impervious to the doctrines of Christ. The Rev R T BROCKMAN said that if ever the Church of England was to be in any real sense the hand-maiden for the re-union of Christendom,it was above all things essential that through her length and breadth she should be utterly and wholly Catholic, not only in intention hut in fact. Tested by her formularies,bywhich alone she should be judged, the Church of England was Catholic in intention and in tact. She had no des tine- tive doctrine but proclaimed the faith of undivided Christendom. The Church in England had never apostasised from the Catholic religion or rejected the Catholic faith. "We have nothing to do with the Reformers," continued Mr Brockman "We have not the smallest desire to be reckoned their follower." He maintained that, what- ever changes were accomplished at the Reformation, the fact stood out clearly that in her formnlaries they bad the Catholic faith put before the children of the Church as their inalienible heritage. Protestantism had no real foothold or position in the formularies of ' the Church. Its true home was outside the borders of I the Church of England, as it was outside the borders of the rest of the Catholic Church. He believed that in future ages when the history of the Church could be written by those who saw from a distance what was happening to-day, it would be said that there had been no more marvellous recovery in any local Church in the history of the Catholic Church than that which was taking place in the Church of England to-day. He believed it was an illustration of the marvellous vitality of Catholicity. In spite of the terrible history of the past 300 years, in spite of Erastianism, in spite of tbe terrible wave of Protestantism which seemed for a time to sweep away everything which made her glorious, in spite of the deadness, slackness and dull ness of the days gone by, the marvellous recovery which they saw taking place in the Church of England to-day as surely a splendid evidence and proof of her inherent Catholicity. The speaker concluded by urging the importance of aggressive work in the spread of Catholicism and declared that if the Church of England failed to rise to the height of her calling she must not be surprised if her mission in this land was carried out by the Church of Rome. The RECTOR (Rev Canon Willis), said they were indebted in one sense—and he had always wished to acknowledge it—to the Roman Communion for their teaching, because it had led them to accept much more Catholic truth than perhaps they would otherwise have been inclined to. On the other hand, he abhorred the presence of the Church of Rome in this country because it prevented many from:becoming truly Catholic. The present Roman Church in this country dated only from the year 1570. "We are the representatives" declsred the Rector"of the old Catholic Church of the land, and we have to rise to the occasion and show that we do know and wish to teach to the utmost of our power the truths of the Catholic Choral."

_ St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street. Special choir services were held at this church on Sunday, when the preacher was the Rev Dr Wilson. of Liverpool. In the morning he took for his subject "The Church the Kingdom of God," and in the evening "The Liturgy of the Blessed Sacrament." The music of the mass—Gounod's " Messe Solennelle "—was very imprersively rendered by the choir. At the evening service the vespers were sung in English. The music also included the Magniticat to a setting by J Danby, the Jubilate Deo from Haydn's " Stabat Mater," " 0 Salutaris" (Elgar), a seventeenth century setting (unaccompanied) of "Penis Angelicus "and " Tantum Ergo" to a Gregorian chant, the whole being very beautifully interpreted. Mr W Wallington presided at the organ and his playing brought out to the full the fine tone and splendid resources of the instrument. M: J Eckersley ably conducted.

St. John's Presbyterian Guild.

There was an excellent attendance and much discus- sion at the me3ting of the above society last Monaay evening. The proc edings took the form of a " Hat Night with Mr D Sutherland presiding. All sorts and conditions of questions were dealt with from " How to improve our society " to "The solution for Labour Untest." '1 he most animated discussion, how- ever, centred round the question of •• Equal pay for equal work" and the many phases of this subject were shown by the Dumber of speakers, the ladies holding no half-hearted views on the question.

St. John's Presbyterian Church.

Preaching at St. John's Presbyterian Church, Wilderspool Causeway on Sunday evening the Rev NV Reid spoke of the Hebrew writers showing under the figure of the eagle making and afterwards destroying her nest, God's ways of providing a safe and suitable home for man in his days of helplessness, and of man's consequent conceptions of comfort and safety as reposing in fixity of surroundings. Of God's invita- tions and encouragements to development and strengthening of character, often frustrated until the relied on circumstances are broken on and in the moment of charge the soul, finds itself. Very beautiful. said the preacher, is the figure of the eagle driving her young into the unknown tcrial depth, and swooping beneath them with outspread pinions to safeguard them—picture of the everlasting arms.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

The usual meeting was held on Sand ty, presided over by Mr William Beswick. Mr O'Callaghan, of Man- chester, a representative of the Anti-Vivisectionist Society, was the speaker. He referred to V-e teaching of science upon life, and to the recent speech on evolution by a prominent member of the BritiFh Association, who, by the way, was a vivisectionist. In their operations upon the dumb animal, they professed to be in search for that which was good for man. He as an Anti-Vivisectionist, strongly opposed that. It was contrary to the idea of the leading professions. God never intended those poor creatures of His to be punished. The process was cruel and inhuman, and was not necessary for the good of man. Mr W Weave and Mr Healey also took part in the meeting. One new member rece,ved a hearty welcome. The President announced that the Rev T I' James, of Sheffield, would give the address to-morrow.

Cairo-street Chapel, Should the Workers Control Industry ?

On Sunday night Dr Mellor brought to a close hi, series of special sermons on " Religion end democracy?' The subject was "What can we do " ? and the chapel was crowded to its utmost capacity. The great thing, the preacher said, was to compel men and women to a realization of the evils of their social life, end to force them to recognise that the whole system of living was fundamentally wrong.. The gospel of discontent had to be preached on every possible Occasion end in every possible way. Public opinion had to he educated ; at present it was sadly in the dark because it refused to see that not merely the details of social life needed remedy but the whole system must be completely changed. The present economic order must be destroyed. All serious men and women, Dr. Mellor sill, could in some way or other preach the Gospel and spread the new tiuth. They could educate their children and their young people in new ways and give them new ideas One of the most important things the workers them- solves could do was to organise more and more com- pletely, not only with a view to raising wages, but also, and far more important, with a view to controlling industry, and so destroying the present order. Inspiration was needed by every worker for the future, and the churches ought to supply this inspiration. They ought clearly to state their opposition to the Whole present system of industry and boldly stand for a new method, teach- ing the rising generation the principle of collectivism and fellowship in material as well as in spiritual things Dr Mellor promised another course of special sermons

in the early part of next year on " Leaders of Revolt," and also aunounced his intention or giving some week evening lectures on Socialism.

Foreign Missionary Anniversary. Religion the Chief Topic in India. As each year passes by we are becoming more and more acquainted with the noble work which is done by those ministers who leave their native land to go and enlighten these who are not so fortunately situated es ourselves for the acquiring of Christian knowledge. These men often at great personal risk open out a new work to the heathen aild they are deserving of everY praise and encouragement in their trying work. Special services were held on Sunday at the Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church in connection with the foreign missionary anniversary and on Monday evenings public meeting was held in the cherch at which the principal speaker was the Rev W H Soper, of Southport, * returned missionary from India. The MAYOR (Councillor Dr Joseph) presided over a fair attendance and in introducing the deputation said that at a time like this it naturally struck him how great was the debt they owed to those really noble men—the ministers of God, who carried on His work at home and abroad, in holding up the banner of Christ „, and keeping the lamp of Christianity burning under very difficult and trying circumstances. There were two bands of those men—those at home and those abroad and each of them had their difficulties. The ministers in this country, whilst socially they might be more comfortable and living under British laws and a better system of sanitation, yet had a great difficulty in fighting against sin and infidelity. They were met that evening, 'however, to consider the second band, those who worked in foreign countries. He knew they must not only have to fight against infidelity, error and sin but they had also to put up with great privation and hardships which the miniqters at home had not to experience. In the first p ice they were banished from their friends and that was a great act of self-denial and for that they were all the more worthy of being sup- ported. One of the reasons, he thought; why those missionary movements were not supported as they ought to be, was due in a great rneasure to selfishness. There were many degrees of selfishness and they should be mastered at the first opportunity. The wider their sympathies were the more Christ-like they became. It had been said that charity began at home but it should not end there—(Hear, hear,)—and he thought all those present would support the foreign missionary movement. He thought it people were less selfish they would be happier themselves. There were two specific reasons why they should extend their thoughts and energies to missionary move - meats as far as they could. 1 hey were com- manded to " Go ye into all the world and preach the Gospel to every creature." That still held good to-day, and they should support those who went out 511 over the world preaching and teaching. The other reason was " Do unto others as you would that they should do unto you." The poor heathen were appeaiing to us for help, and he thought it was our bounden duty to do our utmost to lighten their darkness. (Applause.) The Bev W H SOPER dealt at some length with the work that was being done in India, end said that to-day they had in Hyderabad, the circuit where he bad been, 15,00 of a Christian community. Everyore talked about religion 10 India, but in England it was bard to get them 'to converse upon the subject. Every morning when a Hindoo woman swept round her door she sang to her god. No merchant in India would think of making uP his accounts without making a prayer, and the bullo°k. drivers all prayed to their gad. It was all outside and merely formal but there it was, and o,ly waiting fef. the touch of the Master's hand to quicken it to tee life. The people were pious, lovable aJd teachable. The longer they lived amongst them the longer theY wanted to remain and his heart would always be with; them. A Mohamedan friend of his had once reproechen the people of a village for allowing Christian teaching to eo on. " You are allowing these boys to hf, saturated with Christianity," he said. "Well, well, was the reply, "It is good. It will do them no harm. (Applause.) A Parsee friend of his had said, " Yrnir Christian teachers are coming upon us like carrion kite° upon the dead." The carrion kites, explained th,e speaker, devour the dead bodies of the Parsees whic. were exposed on a high pinnacle. This man once burst into tears because his daughter had fallen into the power of a Mohamedan, and he besought his (I4r Soper's) assistance, as there was no other religion le India which cared for the fallen. A Hindooo priest .once came to him and requested M._ to help them to set up a stone pillar front of a new Hindoo temple in the village. The Hindoo treated him with the greatest courorf and he feared he was very rude ef:er lie had said have come to knock those pillars down. He was almee; sorry be said it, for the Hindoo was more polite t..blia ever. Now, however, in that neighbourhood they more than a thousand Christians, and one temple 11' actually been pulled down, and alongside ran Os! foundations of a little Methodist chapel. (Applanse,f They were trying to bring people to a knowledge, :0 GOO through the Lord Jesus Christ, and he appenle' the congregation to support their work in every w_l'a they could. (Applause). The Rev W A Beckett re7d the annual report on the missionary work which Bho for that the Warrington Circuit had raised £293 13s. 4d. ?ft the cause, and of that sum Bewsey-road contribnlrs' £26 Gs. 2d. vote On the proposition of the Rev IV A BECKETT of thanks was passed to the Mayor for presiding. His WORSIIIP briefly responded.

NOTES BY "MUST."

Well done, Wirepullers! It was a good perform- ance to beat the Saints at Knowsley Road on Thurs- day, and showed that the form in the Cup-tie was all wrong. On that occasion Warringion gave the game away by adopting defensive tactics when they should have been attacking. On Thursday they started very badly, but made the pace a " cracker " in the second half.

There was only one team in it during the first 30 minutes. St. Ilelens had the wind and slope and out- played the Warrington men to such an extent that -they only twice crossed the centre line in half-an-hour, .and on both occasions from penalty kicks. During this spell the Warrington forwards did not count at all, and with endless opportunities the Saints ought to have made their position secure. But while they threw the ball about with much freedom and some skill, they were poor finishers and the Warrington line had some remarkable escapes. Eventually Flanagan got over .at the corner, after Turtill had opened the score with a penalty goal. The " blooding " of the AV irepullers seemed to give them the necessary stimulus and the forwards woke up to such an extent that the last ten minutes were spent in the vicinity of the Saints' line. Twice Warrington should have been over but luck was against them, and the interval came with the Saints leading 5 points to nil.

This was not much of a margin for Warrington to face, and they at once showed that they meant busi- ness. Fearnley just failed to catch the judge's eye in the first minute, and then a siege of the home line commenced. One or two easy penalties were missed, and then Thomas tried a drop-kick which sent the bah E ailing over the bar. A fine breakaway by Rushton gave Dickerson a gallop to the line, and Brooks put the ball over, Thomas kicking a wonderful goal from

touch. This gave Warrington the lead, only for them to lose that five minutes later through a fumble by Jolley, and a neat penalty goal by Turtill. Warrington put " all in " on the attack now and eventually Mclntyre burst over at the corner after robbing the Saints halves of the ball from a five yards scrum. St . .

ilelens _made a poor show this half Wand were beaten

fore and aft. - There was a curious incident in the last minute. Skelhorne and Cox followed up a punt and the former was obstructed by Turtill. C(,x snapped up the ball and raced over the line, only to be recalled by the Referee, who had seen the obstruction, and awarded Warrington a penalty kick. What an award, and what a decision! St Helens' offence robbed War- rington of five points. Luckily the match was won, but the extra points were fully the due of the team, and the try should have been allowed.

Coming to personal matters, the Warrington for- wards were the least satisfactory part of the team, and rarely got possession from the pack. In the first half they were hopelessly out-pointed,and it was only staying Dower that gave them chances in the second half. More power is wanted in the pack before the team can be considered well-balanced. Both halves were ragged in method to begin with, but improved when they changed places. Rushton made a couple of brilliant runs from the base of the pack, and beemed to come out of his shell all at once. The Warrington threequarter line was powerful, though both ,centres kicked over much. Dickenson showed that his rest bad done good, for he was much speedier than usual. In the rear Jolley played his worst game and mulled catch after catch in disheartening style. Be has struck a bad patch and on Thursday's form will have a difficulty to keep his place. There has been a deterioration in his play for some weeks. -- St Helens seem to have been indulging in too many changes and the team was disorganised from start to finish. Much of the haphazard play was no doubt due to men being in unaccustomed places. Turtill was a failure both at threequarter and full back, and the best men behind were Browning and 111,,lyneux. The " A" team will have the smart Wigan Ilighfield aS visitors. Perhaps our local lads will find their form, and record ths first combination victory at home. One or two of the Highfield lads are objects of interest to the Warrington selection committee.

• The "A " team will be somewhat changed through injuries, and the selected thirteen are: Horrocks, lull back; Clare, Dickenson, Jordan and Davies, three-quarters; Brown and Daintith, half backs; Critchley, Bevan, Arnold, Naylor, Harmer and Cooper, forwards.

Warrington " A" team are the puzzle of the season. $o far they have not won a single Combination match at home, but they have beaten Wigan, Salford, and Swinton away. These three performances would, ordinarily, stamp the juniors as being distinctly classy," were it not for the displays at home. I saw the " A" team lose to 8t Helens "A " last Saturday, and a more hopeless exhibition I have not seen for a long time, yet, on paper, the home side was one of the strongest turned out for some time. The two evergreens, Jordan and Naylor, were the only two 'teen who seemed to have an atom of dash in them. liudson was unluckily hurt early in the game—a mis- fortune which settled the question of promotion to the •faeniors yet awhile.

4 The greatest weakness of the team seems to be 1 'forward. The Club was, surely, never represented by ''a worse pack. They are certainly on the light side, I3ut it is lack of football and not lack of weight which ' I.s the chief trouble. The forwards seem to have no v idea of getting possession, and as this is one of the Weaknesses in the senior team, the problem is a dis- quieting one. No doubt the backs in both teams twould show up better behind stronger for.7ards. At ; least two more forwards with both weight and pace -, are a necessity. Warrington's cup-tie prospects will ~ !oon be nipped in the bud unless they get more power Iri the pack. Pace is not everything.

Warrington Committee men were at Whitehaven ' as week end watching the Cumberland-Cheshire "inatch. They had a special ey on one of the Cumber- land three-quarters, but were not impressed with his ability. A correspondent writes to say that the same - 'Player is picked to oppose the Springboks at Gosforth ,text Wednesday, but that does not make him Northern nion class.

Warrington at St. Helens. Fine Win in Second Half.

This postponed league match took place at St Helens on Thursday in damp weather. Warrington supporters travelled in fair numbers by special train and there was crowd of about 3,000. The home-side had made drastic changes, the most important of which was the playing of Turtill, the noted full-back, on the wing. Warrington turned out as selected. The teams were as follows :

St Helens : Molyneux, full-back; Turtill, Green- wood, Belshaw, Flanagan, threequarters ; Browning and Richardson, half-backs ; Butterfield, Mavitty, Heaton, Waddell, Shalicross and Manchester, forwards. Warrington: Jolley full-back; Brooks, Dickenscn, Tranter, and Mclntyre, threequarters ; Nicholas, and Rushton, half-Lacks ; G Thomas, Cheaters, R Thomas, Cox, Skelhorn and Ft arnley forwards. Referee: Mr Renton (Humalet). Warrington lost the toss and the Saints started with a good breeze at their backs. G Thomas set the ball rolling,and Nicholas mulling the home forwards started passing and though the Lail was forwarded Flanagan was only stopped at the corner flag. Nicholas relieved and tbe next minute Greenwood tried a penalty shot at goal which fell short. The home forwards next dribbled, down speedily, and Jolley missed the ball, but I Brooks was handy and saved close in. Then the War- rington backs got away, but Dickenson kicked when he 11 should have passed, Turtill returning with interest. I Warrington were hard pressed, but Skelhorne broke up a St Helens passing run, and Dickenson cleared with a I Penalty. Greenwood mulled, and Warrington got into the home quarter for the first time. Then Turtill knocked on with an open line. Jolley was collared close in and a dead ball :elieved the situation. Again St Helens attacked and Flanagan threw forward on the ' line. So far the Wirepullers had not had a look in, and Turtill opened the score with a penalty goal. At last Warrington got to the other end, only for Cox to get off-side a yard from the live. Again St Helens raced down and Tranter marked right on the line—a brilliant save. Then Turtill was carried over the corner flag by Jolley from Richardson's pass. Keeping it up, the Saints sent across to the other wing, and Flanagan raced over, Turtill failing at goal. Up to now the Warrington forwards had been invisible as far as scrummage work went, and nothing but fumbling by the home backs kept the score down. Then Warrington rushed up, and Dickenson broke through and passed to R Thomas, who was collared a yard from the line. Jolley dropped at goal the next minute, the ball passing under the bar Again Warrington attacked and Dickenson claimed a penalty for obstruction' Thomas failing at goal right in front. Half-time: St. Helens 5 points, Warrington nil—

THE COMBINATION.

WARRINGTON "A" V. ST. HEMS "A."—The wire pullers put a strong second team in the field against St Helens " A," and there was a crowd of about 3,000. The locals early showed up well on the attack and St Helens being penalised, Critchley placed a capital goal St Helens retaliated with vigorous forward play and carried the game to the Warrington line. Scrummag- ing here saw the Warrington men beaten for possession and Trentwith dodged over smartly. Mosedale failed at goal. On resuming the Warrington men made a gallant effortlind Douglas dribbled over but failed to touch down. Hudson was hurt in a tackle and had to retire from the game. Half time : St Helens 1 try. Warrington 1 goal. The second half saw St. Helens have nearly all the play, the forwards completely dominating the scrummage. Trenwith early sent Scholea over fora try but DO goal followed, and the locals struggled desperately to improve matters. The combination of the backs was poor, and Warren twice missed a pass a few yards from the St Helens line. Strong play by :Jordan kept St. Helens on the defence to the and, but Warrington were unable to score again, and retired defeated by 2 tries (6 points) to 1 goal (2 points).

LANCASHIRE CUP FINAL TO BE PLAYED

AT SALFORD.

At a meeting held in Manchester on Monday evening, the Lancashire County Northern Union Committee decided to play the final tie of the Senior Cup Competi- don between Rochdale Hornets (the holders) and Wigan at Salford, on November 30th. Alr R Robinson, of Bradford, was appointed referee, and Messrs T G Hutchins (Oldham) and H Lowe (Broughton), touch judges. The selection of the Willows ground will give much satisfaction in the Weaste district. JUNIOR CUP DRAW.—RYLANDS AT HOME. A draw for the fourth round of the Junior Cup resulted :

WIDNES ALLIANCE LEAGUE. lIELSHV ST. PAUL'S V GREAT SANKEY.

These teams met at Helaby on Saturday. The locals won the toss and T Cook started for Sankey. Play from the outset was fast, the visitors just failing to take the le id. E Darlington getting possession showed a clean pair of heels to T Abbot and put in a high centre, which E Foster beaded into the far corner of the net. E Foster headed a centre against the cross-bar, which T Cook had no difficulty in netting from the rebound. Interval : Great Sankey, three goals ; Helsby St. Paul's nil. THE KING AND QUEEN BOWLING

CLUB.

The annual prize distribution in connection with this successful club took place on Saturday evening. A bot-pot was well served by the proprietor, Mr Frank Starkey. The President of the Club (Mr Thos. S Steel) occupied the chair, and was supported by Messrs F Starkey (treasurer), J W Davies (secretary), Arthur Jolley, J Locker and other members of the committee.

Mr Fred Clare submitted the toast of "The Club," coupling with it the name of the Treasurer. Mr Starkey said they had had a fairly successful season in spite of the unsettled weather, but they were looking forward to a better season next year, when he hoped their membership would be greatly increased. Mr John Monks submitted the toast of" Our President," and Mr STEEL in responding said he hoped that another year they would get higher up in the league, and that they would be able to adorn their clubroom with some of the cups which were open for competition. He cautioned the members to be careful in introducing new members, as it was desirable to keep their membership of working men as select as possible. During the evening songs were rendered by Messrs. J Monks, R Savory, A Jolley, Geo Clifford, W Boscowe, and W Locker.—The following is a list of the prize winners :—Ways and means maio.-1, W Beardsall ; 2, J W Davies; 3, J Monks; 4. W Ashton. Opening main. 1, W Walker; 2, J W Brown ; 3, J Monks ; 4, A Jolley; 5, F Boscowe ; 6, F Clare. Committee main.—1, J W Brown ; 2, J Monks ; 3, A Jolley ; 4, W Ball. THE CHURCH AND DIVORCE. The Archbishop of Canterbury, speaking in Convocs lien on Wednesday, expressed strong disapproval of the majority report of the Divorce Commission, and said even the minority report did not represent what was the view of the Church on the matter. Viscount Halifax, speaking at a meeting of the English Church Union in London, and dealing with the reports of the Divorce Commission, described the proposal to grant divorce by mutual consent as the promotion of free love, an'd said the logical conclusion of divorce on the ground of imprisonment was the establishment of a lethal chamber. He urged the need to work for the entire abolition of divorce.

HOCKEY.

LATCLIFORD.

The First Eleven—in-We--a—Cli;v of 2-2 with Bootle last week, after one of the best games of the present season. Hale,tbe Bootle centre who is a prolific scorer, gave his side a two goals lead, the second goal coming after a brilliant individual run. The lead was reduced by Banks before the interval. The second half was con- tested at a fast pace and, although Latchford made strenuous efforts to equalize it was not until about five minutes off time that Pearson did the needful with smart shot at close range. The visiting forwards were a warm lot and a better line than Latchford's. They found the halves a lot of work and Wilkinson held Bale in the second half, while Enight played a very clever game. Fletcher end Billington were good at heck and stopped many ominous movements of the Bootle forwards.

The Second Eleven were at Manchester Bniversity'a excellent ground at Fallowtield and won a free snort- log game by 3-1 made doubly enjoyable by the strictly impartial decisions of the home umpire %Io took the whole field. The University centre-half and goalkeeper were prominent but their forwards, although doing alright in mid-field, finished badly in the circle. For Latchford Bradshaw awl Banks played resourcefully at half-back and at back Eccles and Dixon cleared well. the latter sending the ball away in no half-hearted manner. Eccles has now become a most reliable back. Corlton was successful at centre-forward, distributing the ball well and scoring two goals. ftobin scored the other goal and played a neat game, as did also Marsden at left inside. The forwards got in some really good passing. The team is settling down and should now go ahead.

The First Eleven play Alderley Edge at Alderley to-day, and their team is :—H W right, goal; W Fletcher, C Billington, backs; F W Isherwood, 3 B Wilkinson, A Knight, halves ; R A Dale, J Petireon, D Travis, E Banks, A Starkey, forwards. CROSFIELD'S CLUB. Crosfield's were at home to Sale Moor last Saturday. The home team opened very strongly and good com- bination amongst the forwards brought them an early goal, which was followed up by several other good attempts. After ten minutes play the home centre-half (H G Sandy) retired owing to injury, this disorganised the home team and Sale centre torward with a brilliant shot, netted the ball,making the half-time score I-1. The visiting team opened the second half in the some Ray as Crosfield's did the first, the loss of Crosfield's centre-half making a great difference. Salo Moor soon got into their stride and the splendid combination of the forwards enabled them to add to their score. Crostield'a, although badly handicapped, managed to add another goal. At this stage another misfortune happened to the home team, E Paget, the right back, receiving an injury which practically put him hors de combat. Now the visiting team had it all their own way, the result being 6.2 in favour of Sale Moor, A C Bloomer and B T Dickson scoring for Crosfields.

Next week a mixed match has been arranged with Miss Brcadbent's Eleven and the team is as follows : A Mellor, goal ; Miss Holland, J H Harrison, backs ; Miss Hill, A Hodson, Miss Marsh, half backs ; B T Dickson, Miss A Jackson, A C Bloomer, Miss E Jackson, A B Lythgoe, forwards.

Exciting Scenes at a Fire. Exciting scenes were witnessed at a fire at the Chapel house (belonging to the trustees of the Wesleyan Chapel), 4, The'wall Lane, on Wednesday afternoon. A clothes maiden, full of clothes, which had been placed round the kitchen fire, was ignited by a spark. Mrs. Ditehfield (mother of the chapel keeper) and her two grand children were in the sitting- room. Hearing the shouts of neighbours, Mrs. Ditchfield rushed out through the kitchen, her hair being scorched.. The children were in a perilous position in the sitting•room, as there appeared to be no means of escape, but a man released them by breaking the windows and entering the room, which was full of smoke. The ages of the children were a few months and five years respectively. When the fire brigade arrived, neighhours had extinguished the flames. Damage to the extent of £4O was done.

President Woodro PRESIDENT Wilson's attitude tO" WILSON'S wards the tariff is the BIG TASK, subject of much mis- understanding. Tll i 5 is partly due to the confusion of terms, for the American Democrats who propose te lower the Customs duties are called Tariff Reformers, which is the name adopted by the party in our own country who wish te increase the number of duties. Further, it is quite true to say that very few of the American Tariff Reformers care to use the words Free Trade, and to that extent there i 5 good ground for saying that there is no Free Trade party in America comparable with the, partk of the same name here. Yet, amid ah this confusion, one thing is clear. It i 6 admitted by journalists on both sides, betel and in America, that there will be graduri' reductions in the United States Custonls duties. When it is remembered that the process of reduction of Customs dutiee covered a period of no less than forty Year: in our own country, it is possible to realii` what a difficult and protracted task lies before President Wilson and his supporters: Their tariff schedules are far more complicated than ours were, and their industries are 0161 more deeply affected by them, especiallY hY the way in which they assist the establish; ment of trusts and monopolies. Thee task n. revision is so great, almost superhuman' Irl fact, that no political party in America dare approach it without the extremest cS.atiebrl; This is thoroughly realised in America the business men and the great commerciee and financial organisations; and, as we hetlo seen, the return of a President pledged lower the tariff has not prejudiced e commercial outlook at all. Probably' t,ll lopping off of the unhealthy limbs, and tne steady reduction of the scheme to PrePer of limits, suitable to the changed conditions the United States, will have a decided'', ThP beneficial effect on the country. of indeed, is the firm expectation and bop@ 4 the new President; who with both 14°115e,, prepared to assist him, should have no dill"' With- culty in clearing the way for progress out friction.

What has happened . to

A VOTE OF the financial resolution COMPLETE over which the Opposi- CONFIDENCE. tion has strewn the shattered fragments of Parliamentary self-respect? It has been withdrawn, with the Banbury amendment attached. That amendment went to its grave, " unhonoured, unsung" 'by even a single member of the Opposi- tion. And a new financial resolution was put forward, differing in form from the original because provisions in two clauses of the Home Rule Bill have been inserted in it, but neither infringing,impairing, nor modify- ing the financial machinery of the home Rule. And this new resolution has been carried by a Government majority of 111. A most effectual vote of confidence in the Government policy!

In the debate on the A " UNIONIST " new financial resolu- THREAT. tion to the Home Rule Bill, the outstanding feature was the threat used by Mr. Austen Chamberlain and Mr. Bonar Law that a " Unionist " Government, if Home Rule was carried,would cancel Irish Old Age Pensions. We make grants to the Transvaal (this should have been of special imterest to Mr. Austen Chamberlain) and to Uganda, said Mr. Lloyd George, but an outcry is raised by the Opposition if there is to be any outgoing from the Imperial Exchequer to Ireland. "We do not give Old Age Pensions to Uganda," exclaimed Mr. Bonar Law. The implication was patent. Unless Ireland as a whole agrees to the views of one-half of Ulster as to the way in which Irish domestic affairs should be governed, a " Unionist " .Government will dock the Irish people of their Old Age Pensions ! This is a striking I example of the "Unionist" view of the means to be taken to consolidate the Empire.

It is well to be off with "ON " AND " OFF " the old pledge before PLEDGES. one is on with the new! Lord Landsdowe gave a "two-fold undertaking" at the Albert Hall. Mi Balfour once gave a two- fold pledge. One pledge was that, whatever increase in the cost of living Tariff Reform might make, the consumer should be com- pensated by the remission of other burdens. The other pledge was a Tariff Reform Refer- •endum.Lord Lansdowne gets rid of the Refer- endum pledge and substitutes a statement that the electors will be told what " limits " the Tariff Reformers will observe in taxing food. For Mr. Balfour's cost-of-living pledge he substitutes a pledge that the revenue from food taxes shall go to relieve the con- sumer's "other burdens." A very different kind of pledge, and by no means "a business proposition," unless Lord Lansdowne con- strues business" as "sharp practice."

Food-taxes raise the TORIES AND FOOD prices over the whole TAXES. area of food—foreign, colonial and home.- grown. Lord Lansdowne and Mr. Bonar Law propose to compensate the consumer for the increased cost of living by the mere amount of the food-taxes which goes into the Exchequer. On the basis of Mr. Chamberlain's "Tariff Reform Commission" scheme the food-taxes revenue would be some eight millions. The increase in the cost of living to the consumer would be some eighteen millions. If Lord Lansdowne thinks that the working classes will accept as "a business proposition" the proposed compensation of eight millions of remissions in return for a burden of eighteen millions upon the cost of living,it only proves once more how thorough is the contempt of the Tory mind for the democratic intelligence. And Mr. Bonar Law expects to reassure both the working classes and the manufacturing interest against any alarm at the increase in the cost of living, and the cost of what must be reckoned, for the purposes of our manu- facturing interests, as raw materials, by the announcement that he is really a conserva- tive-minded man!

NOTES AND NWl.,,yi

Heatley is becoming famous. To the well-worn simile " like carrying coals to Newcastle," Councillor Henshall, at the prize distribution on Wednesday evening, added the original alternative "or like carrying salt to Heatley."

* 0 * "Of course, I would not doubt the veracity of a J.P.," commented the Rev. A. M. Mitchell at the Board of Guardians on Wed- nesday, when referring to a rather startling statement by a fellow-Guardian who occu- pies a seat on the Borough Bench. "Voracity !" unfeelingly interjected another member who apparently has views of his own as to the attributes of the "great unpaid." • * IThe poll for the election for workmen's , representatives on the Court of Referees to ' be set up in connection with the unemploy- ment section of the National Insurance Act .disclosed great apathy on the part of the workers in the Warrington area, only 446 •out of an electorate of between 5,000 and 6,000 recording their votes. This was not , as surprising as it might appear on the • surface, as the benefits of the Act are not yet ; being administered and the insured workers have not had time to realize the importance •of the functions of the Court of Referees. * ... *

- * - Mr. E. England is apparently determined to maintain his reputation as a politician of ' unsettled convictions. At the meeting of the Debating Society on Thursday evening he trounced Mr. Lloyd George's Insurance Act as a "lifeboat very badly manned and I 'equipped" but in the next breath hailed it as a means of saving thousands of lives and , 'said the memory of its author would be • 'revered for generations. No wonder that the 2 member for St. Pancras was forced to confess , that Mr. England was "one of the most delightful specimens of contradiction I have - ever met:' .

* * * The interesting suggestion has been made that Sir William Lever might devote Stafford House to the uses of Prime Ministers as an official residence. Not all Prime Ministers have lived at the famous house in 1 Downing-street. Peel kept his London house i' and used Downing-street as an office. The /Duke of Wellington lived at Apsley House. • Lord Grey lived in Downing-street, but Lord 'Salisbury lived in Arlington-street, and held his Cabinets at the Foreign Office. It would , 'seem that, from the resignation of Lord -. ,Grey, the house was rarely occupied officially 'Until Lord Palmerston installed his private ,secretary there. In Mr Gladstone's first . Administration the same arrangement was •adopted, Mr. Gladstone living in Carlton • Rouse-terrace, while Sir Algernon West 'occupied the Downing-street house. The Downing-street house was given originally 't.o Sir Robert Walpole, who refused to accept ; It as a private gift, but dedicated it to the ,uses of First Lord of the Treasury. It has Many great memories and associations. Most of Mr. Gladstone's great measures took ahape there.

The "Daily Mail" is noted for great dis- coveries. Not very long ago it trumpeted forth the news that there was only one bread —standard bread—and all other so-called breads were stones (more or less). After a seven days wonder and much perturbation among millers (as evidenced in the advertise- ment columns of the "D. M.")the excitement died down and people returned once more to the white bread of a blameless flour. Sus- picions and " standard " bread died a natural breath together, and the public was allowed to eat what it liked. Just recently the "Daily Mail" discovered that Adrianople had fallen, and the news was quite exclusive. Day succeeded day, and other well-informed journals seemed quite ignorant of this start- ling war-cry. From the latest authentic sources Adrianople appears to be still stand- ing, or still falling—we are not sure which! The" Mail," as usual, anticipated the crash.

A still later discovery startled the public of Warrington yesterday, both by placard and paper. The "Daily Mail" has found us out. We have in our midst the birthplace (or something like it) of "a great move- ment." Yesterday the "special correspon- dent" of our far-seeing contemporary wrote a grandiloquent article on National Service and its pioneers in -Warrington.Some parts of the article are really worthy of reproduction. For instance it begins: If the National service Dtague was not actually born in Warrington it has been largely nurtured in that conveniently situated and up-to-date centre. Much of the new propagandist work comes from here, and the monthly organ of the league is edited locally. In the "monthly organ," itself," The Nation in Arms," the editor's name appears as Colonel A. Keene, D. 5.0., who has no local habitat that we wot of.

* • The article then goes on to relate that "the spade work was heavy and the progress slow,"—apparently until Mr. B. S. Townroe, "a young Oxford graduate keen on questions relating to naval defence. . . . came to Warrington to live with Captain CroA.eld." "It proved a powerful combination" says the special correspondent. Captain Crosfield is a member of the Quaker family that founded the great soap firm, of which he is a director. He is, like his brother, an ex-member for Warrington, a Liberal, and naturally has influence on that side of the political world. After much cogitation we must confess to a certain amount of perplexity, arising from the above. We certainly thought at first that the gallant captain was being endowed with Parliamentary honours " like his brother," but it may be that the hasty and parenthetical style of the halfpenny press is responsible for the confusion. In other respects we have no fault to find with this particular paragraph. Captain Crosfield and Mr. Townroe have, undoubtedly, done much for the cause of National Service. And one of them may,some day, be really entitled to write himself M.P. He is merely hoeing a long and stony row at present!

* * Further on we are told with a confidence that is "Mail"-like and sublime: .

That the league has no antagonism towards the territorials is made clear by the fact that Captain Crosfield is a major in the local battalion aud Mr Townroe is a lieutenant. This is about the easiest "get out" for the League that we have read for a long time. The fact that our local pioneers of compulsory service are both officers of the Territorials completely nullifies the effect of recent utterances by so insignificant a public spokesman as Lord Roberts —at least that appears to be the " D.M." view. Quite a lot of people have thought for some time that the National Service League was guilty of an honest (if mistaken) desire to attend the obsequies of the Territorial force—while the corpse yet lives Lord Roberts' notorious Manchester speech lent colour to the view. He told the Terri- torials that they were an "acknowledged failure," and tried to scare them with the cry that the Germans were coming. As his Lordship may be considered the head and front of the National Service League, it requires all the effrontery of the "Daily Mail" to absolve the League of any animus to the volunteer army in such easy terms. Colonel Bromley Davenport has already repudiated Lord Roberts' unjustifiable attack, and this week Major General Bethune, Director General of the Territorial Force,has also gone out of his way to deprecate Lord Roberts' remarks,and to express his faith in our second line of defence. Another Major-General, Sir Alfred Turner, says in the same connec- tion, "it is extremely, unfair, unpatriotic and dangerous to belittle the Territorial Force." Surely this "belittling " is no part of the "great movement" which, is said to have had its rise in Warrington?

* * * It is a long time since we listened to such an interesting and eminently practical address on education as that which Mr. J. H. Reynolds (late Director of Education for Manchester) gave at the Technical Institute prize distribution on Wednesday evening. Beautiful theories and ideals have, of course, an undoubted value even in this work-a- day world, but it is not sufficient, in an industrial community like Warrington, to sing the praises of education on those abstract lives with which all who have attended prize-givings are familiar. Mr. Reynolds took the right course in emphasising the fact that commercial supremacy depends upon an educated and highly trained people, and in this connection he drew some interesting comparisons with Germany, which were not at all flattering to our methods. Germany has long since realised the necessity not only of continued education for her youth, long after the age at which it too often comes to a sudden stop in this country, but of applying the latest scientific developments to her manufacturing. processes. We can with advantage follow Germany in this direction and, as Mr. Reynolds well pointed out, it is here and not on the battlefield, that the real warfare lies. * * MARY'S LITTLE PLOT. Mary had a little plot, The soil was very poor; But still she kept it all the same, And struggled to get more. She kept the plot until one day The people settled dawn; And where a wilderness had been Grew up a thriving town. Then Mary rented out her plot (She would not sell, you know), And waited patiently about For prices still to grow. They grew, as population came. And Mary raised the rent, With Common food and raiment now, She could not be content. She built her up a mansion fine, Had bric-a-bac galore ; And every time the prices rose, She raised the rent some more. " What makes the plot keep Mary so?' The starving people cry. " Why, Mary keeps the plot, you know," The wealthy would reply. And PO each one of you might be " Wealthy, refined and wise," If you had only hogged some land And held it for the rise. Mary C. Hudson.

A Tory-Socialist coalition ensured a safe passage for the Insurance Bill in our local Parliament on Thursday evening. Having succeeded in getting their amendment incor- porated in the Bill, the Socialists acclaimed it as a vast improvement on Mr. Lloyd George's measure, but most of the workers would probably share Councillor Hawthorn's opinion that it was "an employers' relief Bill." The contributory principle is admittedly the only sound basis on which a scheme of national insurance can be satis- factorily worked, and it is therefore but elementary justice that the employer as well as the workman should pay his quota to the fund.

* * * The indispensable work of the District nurses is so generally appreciated in War- rington, that it is surprising to find, from the annual report of the hon. treasurer (Coun- cillor H. Roberts), that last year the sub- scriptions to the funds of the association fell off to the extent of .£l6 9s. In the coming year the expenditure will be increased by the cost of an additional nurse, whose services are imperatively required to cope with the ever-growing demands upon the associa- tion. It need only be pointed out that during the past twelve months 14,695 visits were paid to 595 cases to show how thorough and widespread are the operations of the associa- tion,and its claim upon the generosity of the charitable is undoubted. The report pays a simple but fitting tribute to the memory of the founder of the association—the late Mr. Frederick Monks—in the words, never more aptly quoted, "He, rests from his labours and his works do follow him."

Warrington Main Burst Owing to the large tank water main bursting in Wilder spool-read on Saturday morning considerable inconvenience was caused •to the residents in that neighbourhood and in the village of Stockton Heath. Before the leakage was discovered it was estimated that many thousands of gallons of water had been wasted, and an adjacent field was partially flooded. A gang of men got to work quickly, but the normal supply was not resumed till Sunday morning, and many residents had to beg water from neighbours who had been warned in time to take in sufficient to meet their requirements for the day. To remedy the leak- age it was necessary to excavate a long length of trench varying from 6ft. to Oft. deep.

Much regret has been occasioned locally, especially in military circles, by the death of Major M A Ord, late of the Ist South Lancashire Regiment. The funeral took place at Basingstoke on Friday of last week. Eight N.C.O.'s of the deceased Major's regiment acted as pall-bearers, and detachments of the Hants. Yeomanry, 4th Hants. Regiment, R.H.A., Boy Scouts and Church Lads' Brigade formed pat t of the procession. The coffin was borne on a gun carriage, covered with the Union Jack, and the deceased's helmet, sword and medals were placed on it. The service was fully choral. Major Ashvvorth represented the 2nd Battalion ; Captsin Cotton repre- sented the Depot; and Lieut. H H Withers the 40th (let Battalion). Those officers named walked immedi- ately behind the gun carriage in uniform. The route taken by the procession was crowded with sym- pathetic onlookers. The C.L.B. sounded the Last Post. Major Ord resigned from his regiment in July on account of ill-health, and died of cancer in the throat. He was exceedingly popular in the regiment, and his early death is greatly deplored. FOR MODERN FIREPLACES and Cooking Ranges com- bining economy with simplicity try J. S. GARLAND, Ironmonger, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. Death of Mr. Ernest E. Woods. .

One who was until a few years ago a prominent personality in local athletic circles passed away at Boughton Monchelsea, Maidstone, on Saturday, in the person of Mr. Ernest Edward Woods, son of Mr Edward Woods, solicitor, Horsemarket-street, War rington. The deceased who was only 38 years of age, had suffered from an internal complaint of long standing, but his death was unexpected. A fortnight ago he was in Warrington, when there was apparently a decided improvement in his health. Mr. Woods was a great enthusiast at cricket and was for many years a member of the Warrington Cricket and Bowling Club. He assisted the town club in many of their first team engagements and was chiefly noted for his aggressive work with the bat, whilst as an outfield his fine turn of speed was invaluable. For one season Mr Woods acted as captain of the second eleven. A few years ago he assisted with distinction the village club, Moore and Daresbury. As a hockey player Mr Woods was associated with the Stockton Heath Club, and his defensive abilities marked him out as one of the most competent full- backs in the district. Mr. Woods also achieved some notable victories on the cycling track a decade ago, but, acting on medical advice, he was compelled to relinquish that pastime.

A fire broke out on the premises of the Whyrnan Foundry Co., Ltd., Dallam-lane at about 8 o'clock on Saturday morning. The Fire Brigade turned out under the direction of the Superintendent and Deputy- Superintendent, and on arriving at the foundry they found a few tons of coke on fire near the one of the furnaces. A good supply of water was obtained and the flames were extinguished in about two hours. The loss is covered by insurance.

The Warrington Philomathic Society. The usual fortnightly meeting of this society was held at the Old Academy, Bridge Foot, on Wednesday evening, when, before a very good attendance of members and friends, Mr M Ashton gave some most enjoyable " Readings from Mark Twain." The readings were prefaced by a few brief but interesting remarks upon the style and characteristics of the famous American humorist. The lecturer commented upon Twain's verbal extravagance, reckless literary courage, and fertility of invention, aud said that he was the high priest and founder of the modern type of humour. Mr Ashton divided Mark Twain's humour roughly into three classes—humour of situation, of pure extravagance, and of the unexpected —giving apt and amusing illustrations of each. In the course of the readings themselves, Twain's delightfully quaint humour and excellent descriptive 'power were skilfully brought out by the lacturer, whose selections were thoroughly appreciated by the audience. The selections were taken from "The adventures of Tom Sawyer's" schoolboy life, to some extent autobiographical; "The adventures of Fluckle- bury Finn," perhaps Twain's finest work; "Roughing it " camp life in the mines; and several other works, all of which were admirably rendered. A short dis- cussion followed, and on the motion of Mr N Nield, seconded by Mr T E Knowles, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mr Ashton for his interesting address.

Death of Mr. John Mclntyre. A prominent agriculturist passed away ou Saturday in the person of Mr. John Mclntyre of Ivy Cottage, Great Budworth, who had attained the age of 81 yea's. The son of a Perth farmer he crossed tbe border upwards of half a century ago, and became the tenant of Belmont Farm, Great Bud worth. under Mr Leigh, for whom he also acted as agent for many years. He was an expert in every section of agricu - tura and his services were often requistioned as judge of produce at various shows. Ten years ago he retired from farming and went to live at Fairfield Cottage, fr( m whence he went to live at Ivy Cottage. He was succeeded at Belmont Farm by his son Mr John Henry Mclntyre. Notwithstanding the calls which his farm pursuits made upon him, the deceased devoted considerable time to public affairs, and represented the township on the old Runcorn Rural Sanitary Authority and the Runcorn Rural Council and Board of Guardians for a lengthy period. For some years the deceased was chairman of the Runcorn Rural Council. At the time of his retirement Mr Mclntyre was the second oldest tenant on the estate, and was widely known and greatly respected. One of his sons—Mr W Mclntyre of Stockton Heath—is at present a member of that authority. He leaves a widow and four sons and three daughters.

Home Heading Union. At a meeting of the Home Reading Union on Tuesday at the Guild of Help offices, Mrs Ransome opened a discussion on social conditions in the three cities of Edinburgh, Oxford and Leeds. These were graphically summarised in a wall chart prepared for the purpose. Side by side were placed facts concern- ing population, mortality, wages, rents, occupations, housing and charities. Among other things attention was called to the special housing problem of Edin- burgh, where some of the great mansions are now-a- days occupied by numbers of separate families of the poorest classes. Oxford conditions were shown to be very similar to those of the sister 1 University Of Cambridge. The great demand 1 for women's work tended to take rpsponsibilities from the shoulders of men. Miss Octavia Hill's scheme of lady rent collectors had done useful work. The system of dealing with tramps had diminished their number. Leeds was found to be a town with a fairly normal death-rate, but a high rate .of infantile mortality. The Notification of Births Act had not been adopted, and there were no health visitors appointed by the municipality. In Leeds children were permitted to leave school at the age of 12, and there was a good deal of unregulated child labour. The Employment of Children Act had not been adopted. Great interest was aroused in the ensuing discussion, especially in filling in local facts on the chart to see how Warrington compared with some other towns.

Church Society Lecture. Under the auspices of the Warrington Church Society, the Rev. Charles Harris (organizing secretary for the Liverpool Cathedral) delivered a lecture in St. Clement's Mission last evening on "Liverpool Cathedral : Its place among the Cathedrals of England," with specially prepared lantern photo- graphs. The lecturer pointed out that national archi- tecture was expressive of national life and character and proceeded to trace the progress of the different styles in a most interesting manner. He said Liverpool was rising to the great opportunity which presented itself of building a Cathedral which would be worthy to rank with the magnificent temples built by our forefathers. In a vivid way he described the work now in progress and pictures of the handsome Lady Chapel, already completed were shown. Mr Harris commended the Liverpool Cathedral Gratitude Fund, which owes its inception to Mr W Stewart Royston, of Paddington, near Warrington. . . _

EXHIBITION AT THE SCHOOL OF ART

The exhibition held at the School of Art this week has served to emphasise the wide scope which the work of this excellent institution now embraces. In days gone by the fame of the school was, to a great extent, confined to painting but changing conditions I have made it imperative that, if the school is to fulfil its purpose in an industrial centre like Warrington, "art for art's sake" cannot be its sole objective, but the practical side most also have attention. Thus it has come to pass that we now have art applied to craftsmanship and industry to an ever-increasing extent—in fact such training forms a more or less important Dart of the instruction of every student in the school. It is to be regretted that the townspeople as a whole have but a shadowy conception of all that the school stands for, and efforts to dispel their apathy do not meet with much encouragement. This week's exhibition, which unfortunately has not attracted the atterftion it deserved, has consisted largely of works sent by Warrington students to the national competition held by the Board of Education at South Kensington. No fewer than 215 schools of art in England were repre- sented and it is a matter for congratulation that Warrington secured several commendations, one bronze medal and two King's prizes. The exhibits included some excellent work in actual material,. carried out in the crafts room from the students' own designs; stencil work, painting, decota tion, embroidery, leather work, beaten metal, enamels, painted vellum, still life painting, charcoal and water colour studies from costumed models, aid modelling in clay. Special attention was attracted by an extensive study rf historical ornament in furniture by S. W. Clatworthy, who also had some very good landscape drawings and a striking design for a gate post in carved stone. It. A. Collinge showed a fine study, shaded in chalk, of drapery arranged on the antique figure. Two embroidered purse bags byH Miss Lowe and Miss Johnson were most tastefully I worked. Fred Jones had an elegant design for a I carved settee and Allan Brooks sent an exquisitely I illuminated address. Some effective work by E B Allen included design for leather book cover, silver enamelling, and hair comb carried out in material. Among the specimens of modelling those of R C Essenhigh, R E Poulton and Miss Shakeshaft stood out prominently. Mr Essenhigh had also admirable designs for a stone corbel, newel post and ceiling boss. Water colour paintings of still- life groups by Miss Pearson and Miss Clare were generally admired, as was also the mosaic panel of a memorial tablet submitted to the Warrington Society by Arthur Hayward. Other notable features were a stencilled design for a frieze by H Fraser; specimens of leather work by Miss Stewart, Miss Lowe, Miss Sbakeshaft and Miss Pearson, a silver cap by E B Allen, a beaten copper bowl by Miss Crampton, a I perforated brass plaque by Miss Harrison, enamels by Miss Hall, Miss Crompton, Miss Clara and E B Allen, a vellum fronted box by Miss Johnson, a carved frame by Miss Shakeshaft and stencils by R E Poulton and Miss Moston.
