Singular County Court Action at Warrington.

An unusual County Court adtion was beard at War- ringtou on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stanger K.C., when Lloyd's Bank Limited sued John Griffiths, Rylands-street, Warrington, and his wife for the return of a cheque for £2B or its value—Mr Hugh Damon (instructed by Messrs Potts, Potts and Gardner, of ' Chester), appeared for the plaintiffs and Mr Burgis (instructed by Mr Josiah Longland, Warrington) repre- sented the defendants. Mr Gamon explained that the cheque for £2B was drawn on Parr's Bank by MID Griffiths lin favour of Walter Dale, who carried on business as a grocer at Kelsall and had had an account with Lloyd's Bank at Chester for several years. His account had not been very satisfactory and in February of this year the bank definitely refused to let him have any overdraft whatever. In the meantime, when cheques were paid in by Dale they were credited to him and he was allowed to draw against them immediately. The cheque in question was paid into Lloyd's bank on August 30th and credited to Dale. It was not noticed at the time that the cheque was post- dated but it had since transpired that the date was an error. The original date intended was August 29th, but the cheque was actually dated September 29th. Dale was allowed to draw upon it and be withdrew practically the full amount of the cheque which was sent on to the clearing house in the usual way. On September 3rd it was returned to Lloyds' Bank at Chester, by Parr's Bank, Warrington, as " dishonoured." When the plaintiffs received the cheque back they at once wrote Dzile, enclosing the cheque, and pointing out that, as he had drawn upon it there was a debit balance on his account of £27 16s. 4d. Learning that the cheque had been forwarded by Dale, to Mrs Griffiths, the bank communicated with her, ask- ing her to correct the cheque and return it. As no reply was received, a further letter was sent on September 18th, whereupon Mrs Griffiths wrote that the cheque was given to Dale as a temporary loan on the strict understanding that he banded back to her £2B in cash within seven days. The cheque was post-dated in error and on returning it to her, Dale intimated that he would be unable to find the cash equivalent, owing to disappointment. She therefore decided not to return the cheque corrected. The case was purely one of law and it lay in a nutshell. He submitted that the bank became holders for value of the cheque and that the property passed to them, because it was received on the basis that it should be drawn against immediately. If his Honour was satisfied that the property became vested in the bank, they could recover the cheque unless the defendants established that not only was the cheque delivered to somebody else, but that it was delivered with the express intention of transferring the property. The onus was on the defendants to show that.

Alfred Irton, manager of Lloyd's Bank at Chester, said Dale's account had been unsatisfactory for two or three years. In answer to Mr Burgis, w'tness said that after the cheque had been draw upon, the bank heard that Dale had made an arrangement with his creditors and that they would have to go in for a composition. Finding that out, you began to think about getting the whole amount from somebody else ?—No, that is not so. You must debit his account when the cheque comes back ?—iNe do not necessarily lose our security in the cheque. Further questioned witness said they presumed Dale had not repaid Mrs. Griffiths, because they knew he had ' not the money. They, however, knew nothing of the arrangement until Mrs. Griffiths wrote them. The cheque was sent to Dale for correction, as it seemed so obvious that the drawer bad simply made a slip of the pen. Although Dale's account had not been satisfac- tory, they never doubted that he would deal perfectly straight with them in the matter. Mr Burgle submitted that the bank were endeavour- ing to play a game of "Heads I win, tails you lose.'. First they looked to Dale, and when they found he was not good for the amount they began to change their tune and tried to look after the drawer of the cheque Under those circumstances, the drawer was entitled to rely upon any legal point there was. The action was In reality brought to recover a sheet of paper. He , submitted that the plaintiffs were not the owner of

MAINTAINING PROTESTANTISM.

The Work of Emmanuel Church.

" We are striving to maintain the Protestant truths of the glorious Reformation," was the Rev. W. T Jaye's summary of the position of Emmanuel (Reformed Episcopal Church) at the opening of the annual sale of work in the Church institute, Kuutsford-road, On Wednesday afternoon. The whole of the work of the Church and Sunday school is maintained entirely by voluntary contributions, without pew rents or endow- ments, sad an annual sale is therefore an urgent necessity. As usual, the ladies of the congregation had worked very hard for the success of the project, and the stalls were heavily laden with the products of their self-sacrificing efforts. The following were the stall-

No 1 Stall.—Mrs Jaye, Mrs Lees and Friends. No 2 Stall—Mrs A (Andy, Mrs Atherton end Friends. No 3 Stall.—(Refreshment) Mrs Booth and Friends. No 4 Stall.—The Sunday School Teachers. No 5 Stall.—(Gentlemen's Stall) Mrs William Bibby, junior, and Mr F Lloyd. No 6 Stall.—(Flower and Fruit), Mrs J Bibby and Friends.

No 7 Stall.—Miss N Gandy and Friends. Tea Roorn.—Mrs Vat. Bibby, sen , and Friends. Tbere was a good attendance at the opening of the F ale by Mr C E Parker, who was accompanied by Miss Parker.

The Rev W T JAYE (curate-in-charge) said they at Emmanuel Church were a peculiar people." They believed they were doing a much-needed work in this town in these days of ritualism and advanced sacredotalism. As an independent church, they were striving to maintain the Protestant truths of the glorious Reformation. They believed that they con- formed to the doctrines of the Church of England with the exception that they considered they would not be doing right if they for one moment countenanced anything which would place man before God and give the people the idea that they must come to God through a human mediator. There was much advanced Cuurchism in Warrington, but, judging by the way in which people were rallying round and by the increased congregations during the last few months, their work at Emmanuel Church was not in vain. (Applause). Mr. PARKER said the self-sacrifice which the sale represented was an evidence that the people con- nected with the church felt there was something worth while working for. They believed that what their Church stood for was worthy of being sustained. He did not suppose that any of their efforts had been put forth for the immediate profit or advantage of those who bad engaged in them, but had been treely given because they believed that the Gospel of the Saviour Whom they served could alone heal the wounds of the world. Their church was a recognition of the fact that there were not only slums in the city, but slums in the heart, which could only be cleansed by the power of Christ. It stood in a Inaterialistic age for great truths, one of which was that here we had no abiding city, but that there was beyond a world, belief in which should regulate our conduct and work here. It, was a pleasure to him to help forward their work in any way and he wished the present effort all success. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr W ATHERTON, seconded by Mr JOHN BIBBy, Mr Parker was heartily thanked. The proceeds on Wednesday amounted to £56 Bs 10d.

The sale was re-opened on Thursday afternoon by Miss Bessie Dutton and last evening by the Rev W T Jaye. Thursday's proceeds realized £34 3s 9d.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNING.

RACE AT LEIGH.

Fifty-six runners took part in a 6,1, mile race over two laps of stiff country in the vicinity of Leigh and Bedford. Leigh Harriers mustered 30, Bolton 15, and Warrington A.O 11, the home team being almost fully represented whilst neither of the visiting teams were up to expectations but nevertheless great interest was taken in the contest which resulted in a win for War- rineton by a narrow margin. GUARANTEE

STEADY WORK

CRICKET LEAGUE PRESENTATION. The Mayor on the Influences of Cricket.

"The king of games" was the title given to cricket at the annual presentation of the shield and medals in connection with the Warrington and District Cricket League in the Roan Cow Hotel on Tuesday evening. The shield and gold medals were won by the Sankey team and the runners up, Messrs. Rylands' team, were presented with silver medals with gold centres. Mr J W Travis (chairman of the league) presided and there were also present the Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Mr J Fulton Smith (one of the vice-presidents), Mr Charles E Hawthorn (hon secretary), Mr C Hackney (hon. treasurer), and members of both teams,aloug with friends.

At the outset the Chairman said that for the Mayor and Mr Smith to come down that night showed that they were in sympathy with the league and the healthy and manly game it promoted. (Hear, hear). He him- self thought cricket was the best. game of the whole series, not exempting football. He always looked on cricket as the king of games and that was why he took such a great interest in it. (Applause). Prior to presenting the awards, the MAYOR said that this year he had to present the medals to a different set of victors lie understood that towards the end of the season 1911 interest in the League was on the wane,so much so that it looked as if the association would have to be dia- -1 solved, but, owing to the admission of new clubs and a revival of interest in the game on the part of the older clubs, things had mended very considerably. Just as the darkest hour was before the dawn, the last season had almost been more successful than any they had had hitherto. The bad weather had fortunately not inter- fered very materially with the matches. He was reminded that last year he encouraged the members of the Sankey Club to persevere still further as they seemed to be very near winning the shield. They had won it this year and be did not know whether it was the result of his prophecy on that occasion or that of Mr T Halton. Referring to the close character of the games, his Worship said he believed that until the last match they coul.i not tell which way the championship was going and that showed how very closely the clubs were matched and must be a sign of excellence. (Hear, hear.) He had seen bowling and cricket matches, but he had never been to a football match. He had noticed with great pleasure how keenly the contests were carried on, even to the last two men, but once the conqueror was declared, the man who had been fighting most vigorously for victory was the warmest in his congratulations. (Applause.) It seemed to him they were quite free from "envy, hatred, malice and all uncharitableness" and he thought that was a good thing. He had been struck with the fact that cricket taught a man to play a straight game. entirely in accordance with the rules, and the habit of being trained in such a manner must necessarily— especially in young people—have an effect in the still more important game of life. (Applause.) Games of that character were looked upon by some people as a waste of time, and merely as an afternoon's sport. He , could see something more than that in them' for he could see they had the effect of euabling a man to form good habits, of obeying regular rules which must have an influence, if properly applied, on their everyday life, That, in his estimation, was a very important thing. (Applause.) The trophy and medals were then presented by His Worship and each individual member of the team was cheered as he received his medal. The iollowing were the winners:—Shield and gold medals, Sankey team;—F Millington (captain). 0 Dick- inson .1 Plant, J Bramhall, T Whitfield, E Sullen, A Corradine. S Bingham, H D‘intith, E Whitfield, S Gregson, E Foster, T C Porter and W Williams. Messrs Rylands team : silver medals with gold centres, H Booth (captain), W Whitley, .1 Sheader, A Bramhall, S Carter' T Newton, G McGowan, F Jolley, A Renwick, E P Sanders, G Gough, T Horrocks, J Waywell, and T Williams.

• The Chairman read a letter of apology from one of the vice-presidents of the league (Mr Thomas nalton)in which he expressed his appreciation of the good sport displayed and also his delight at "good old Sankey ' being the premier club. (Applause.) Mr. J. FULTON-SMITH said that when the trophy fell to the same club year after year the other members of the League were apt to become disheartened. In his opinion there should be a rule that after it had been won by one team for three years, that team should retire for one year—they should not play—and hand over the shield to the winning team for that year. That, be was convinced, would keep up the interest in the game and help the younger members to do their best to secure the honour for their own team. He thought that had been done in Manchester and was working very well. Continuing, the speaker confessed that he had a leaning towards Sankey, and he was highly pleased that they had the honour of winning the shield. He had no doubt that in the days to come Sankey would be the leading district and Warrington the suburb. (Laughter.) He then voiced Colonel Reid Walker's regret at not being able to be present that night, but he always took a great interest in the League. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr. HAWTHORN, seconded by Mr C Hackney a hearty vote of thanks was accorded the Mayor and Mr Smith, and the Mayor in reply said that Mr Smith's suggestion re One team staying out for a year was worthy of consideration, but he thought it would be more satisfactory if it could be done without. A highly interesting vocal and musical programme was disposed of to the delight of all by Messrs W Scarisbrick (whose sketches were very creditably done) W W %Vhittl 0 Gough, J Birch and others. Mr J Stanley, who played several pianoforte solos, was as efficient accompanist.

WARDER BECOMES PRISON GOVERNOR. In succession to the late Mr T W Mackie, Mr.) Ball chief warder of Birmingham Gaol, bas been appointed Governor of the three county prieous at Carmarthen.

WIFE'S RIBS BROKEN.

A sad story of -a-h-u:ba-nd7s-br-ulaiee-n-gmt towards his wife was unfolded at the Warrington Police Court on Monday when before the Mayor (in the chair) and other magistrates. Thomas Kearns, 3, Gandys' Walk was charged with having caused grievous bodily harm to his wife by kicking her. In asking for a remand the Chief Constable said that on Saturday evening prisoner assaulted his wife in a most brutal manner by kicking her most severely. As a consequence she had to go into the Infirmary suffer- ing from broken ribs and other serious injuries. An inspector had been informed by the house sergeou that four or five ribs were broken. The assault was a most cowardly one. The prisoner went home drunk and without the slightest provocation he attacked his wife. He first demanded a oney from his wife to which she was entitled from an Insurance Society her mother having died and his wife had just been to the funeral. On her refusing he kicked her in a most brutal fashion. It was very questionable when they would be ready to go on with the case on account of the wife's injuries. The Mayor : There is some danger to the woman I expect. The Chief Constable: Yes sir. Inspector Forster said he found everything in the house broken to splinters and when he charged the prisoner be replied "I'm saying nowt. She can do what she likes."

The Magistrates' Clerk : Have you anything tn say why the case should not be remanded for a week ? Prisoner : I have a lot to say. lam not going to be tried now.

The Clerk : Are you not ? (Laughter.) The 'Chief Constable explained that two women had brought Mrs Kearns out of the house at groat danger to themselves. The prisoner was then kicking her on the floor.

The prisoner was remanded in custody for a week.

PRISONER COMMITTED TO. THE ASSIZES.

At the tVarrington Occasional Court, yesterday, before Mr G Mather, James Gilbert Whitley, a farm labourer, of Leeds, was charged with setting fire to a hay stack belonging to Mr Thomas Owen, farmer, Vicar's Green, Sankey. on November 13th and doing damage to the extent of £lOO. From the evidence it appeared that the stack, along with others was seen safe at 0.30 p.m. on the day in question. At • 7.10 Mr Owen was called out by a bricklayer's apprentice named Arthur Stretch, Broad Oak Hotel, Sankey, to the stack which was burning furiously. The Warring- ton Fire Brigade and the Sankey Police were then sent for( The lad, Stretch, was standing at the corner of the Liverpool-road about 7 o'clock when he saw a man running from the stacks. He stopped the man and asked

why he was running and prisoner replied inaudibly. The man went away in the direction of Liverpool but he was stopped by Sergeant Smith in a breathless condi- tion. When asked where he was going, prisoner replied "I don't know." He admitted having been at Chestei the previous night and gave the name of James Walker. He was however, picked out of eight other men by Stretch. The officer took the lad to the Sankey Police station where the prisoner admitted setting the stack on fire. He stated that he could not get work and ,he was as well in gaol as out. He knew he was seen and would get caught. He then gave the right name. He was found in possession of a knife but there were no matches. In reply to the charge he said "I am guilty of it." When asked if he had anything to say yesterday morning, he replied " I have nothing to say" The magistrate committed the prisoner to the Liverpool assizes and also complimented the Sergeant for the way in which he had caught the man. The Super intendent said he w ould see the compliment went to the proper quarter.

WARRINGTON BETTING RAID. A fine of £4O and costs Imposed.

At the Warrington Police Court on Thursday before Mr J Rymer Young (in the chair) and otter magistrates Joseph Appleton, 7, Baxter-street, was charged with having used the premises, 7. Baxter street, between the 6th and 16th November, for the purpose of betting, and John Appleton of the above address was charged with aiding and abetting on the 16th November. . _ _

. Mr TS Steel appeared tor the defendants and pleaded not guilty. The Chief Constable explained that the men appeared under the Betting Act of 1353. On Wednesday the 6th Just, a constable passed at the rear of the shop, 7, Baxter-street, disguised, and he mingled with a number of workmen. When near the back door of the premises the officer clearly end distinctly saw the defendant Joseph receive betting slips containing money. At the time John pIS in the yard of the premises. Arrange- ments were made that the house should be kept unoer Observation and this course was adopted,and on the 9th inst. Detective Sergeant Holland and P.C. Skelton watched the premises for a considerable time and between 12 o'clock noon end 2.45 p.m. they saw no less than 112 persons enter the back gate leading into the back yard of the premises. There were 97 men, 11 boys and four women. The officers saw the delendant Joseph go out in the entry and receive from various people no less than 27 slips. The remainder of the people went in the yard and all went towards the back door Irnmedistely the people left .1( s- ph went to the entry and raw them off the pre- mises Pe fore entering the yard a large number of the per r. to in slirs by the defendant's house. Some s oy s were writing out football coupons and they • eat es the scullery door. On the 12th inst. lie • Ives W olacott and Holland kept observations, , t y saw the distendants receive 13.-ttlng slips from • • ...s ,s nod icso 39 men, two buys and a woman • et. the backyard. Joseph Appleton was seen to i•ve a large number of slips from men in the yard, number ef men were seen to go to the back door. . s‘ten 12-20 and 1-20 on Saturday last the house was unds-r observation again, and a number of people vs era seen to visit the premises and go apparently to the hack door and then leave the premises. About 1-25 Chief-Inspector Williamson along with other officers raided the premises. When they went in the shop Mrs Appleton, the mother of the defendants, was thole and when she was asked where her son Joseph was she immediately went to. the back of the shop. When the officers followed they found the door bolted. Subsequently they saw the woman stirring up the fire on which some small pieces of paper were burning. She appeared to be very anxious that something should be lost sight of. A search of the premises was made and a large amount of betting paraphernalia was found— football coupons, payin; out slips and other things. At the time the raid was made the defendant John was seen acting as a scout, running backwards and forwards down the entry, apparently to give information to his brother that nothing was happening. When the raid was made he acted after the style of the horses on the paper—he wes extremely fast. Two young men were found on the premises, apparently for betting purposes at the entrance to the back door.

P.C. Skelton gave corroborative evidence, and said that when he was disguised he could easily have made a bet himself, but he thought it advisable not to do so., By Mr Steel : He was secreted a( me 30 or 40 yards from the bonne in a bedroom at the rear, and on top of some ni+.ttresses.

Mr Steel : -4.:he wall in the entry will be over six feet high ?—Na. I can see over it in places. Mr Steel :I am pretty tall,but I could not see over it. The Chief Constable : He was trying to. (Laughter.) Sergeant Holland deposed to seeing the woman put something in the wash boiler. He found it was a parcel wrapped up in a sporting paper and containing £2 13s in silver and copper. Appleton said the money that was put in the boiler was all he had taken that day. de did not keep the money in his possession. Taff mother bad fainted and the officers were desirous of getting the search over Quickly James Gleave (17), '2l, Powys-street, said he had known the defendant Joseph Appleton for four years. About six months ago he had taken one or two slips to him and be gave him a slip last Saturday. Chief Inspector Williamson said that as they approached the premises, the defendant John ran down the passage and Detective Sergeant Reddingt on pursued him. He told the defendants be had a warrant for their arrest and Joseph replied " I don't use the house I use the entry." With other officers, witness searched the house and they found on the kitchen dresser 23 filled up football coupons and settling no slips, betting rules and a quantity of blank slips, over £6 in cash, a cribbage board and playing cards were found in the home. He asked Joseph Appleton to produce the slips and he replied "You have got them all and the money my mother put in the boiler was taken to-day. I do not keep the Slips on me but take them in the house when I get them." The defendants were taken to the police station in a cab and on the way Joseph said " There are others doing it as well as me, and if you had come next Saturday you would have been too late." Mr Steel said if the men had been charged with street betting he would have pleaded guilty and that would have ended the whole thing. They bad been engaged in street betting and one of the defendants had been convicted of that offence. He was clearly under the impression that if he stood by the yard gate, it was only ordinary street betting. He (Mr Steel) thought the defendant made a mistake by taking the slips in the yard of the dwelling house. He understood that the defendant did intend to give up that practice and would have done so on Saturday (to-day). In regard to the defendant John, the only evidence against him was that he was seen in the passage. He thought that what took place in the passage did not come within the specified section under which both men appeared. The Chief Constable said the police bad not the alighest objection to the case against John being with- drawn after what Mr Steel had said. -

_ . The case of aiding and abetting against John was then disrnisqed. 'rho Chief Constable said defendant had been once previously before the court in 1910 on two charges of street betting and was tined 50s. and costs in each case. If they would give up the practice as they promised, that was all the police desired. The magistrites retired and on their return the Chairman said they had decided to fine defendqnt £4O and costs (£42 6d. in all) or in default three months' imprisonment.

THE LEATHER TRADE IN WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT.

Last week saw a revival in all branches, as all lines moved swiftly, and the shortage of supply was as pronounced as ever. A fair amount of enquiry was still made for contract parcels, and, although this particular business has not received much encourage- ment, it is learned that here and there a small amount of forward business has been done. in most cases primarily wfth the idea of keeping running accounts open. Generally buyers were not pressing to buy forward, although most are with the tanners in the opinion that higher prices will come yet. The difficulty seems to be that the public are not educated to the pmition, as manu- facturers are not even now making the necessary advances on boots, and, further, that what may be called the outside circle of manufacture's have not made any serious attempt to do so. As one of them said : "I he best thing for our trade would be for , tanners to put leather up 2d, at one jump; it would bring the outsiders into line double quick." Whilst this may ba the counsel of perfection, it is certainly true that prices continue to advance, and bend quota- tions are perceptibly stiffer again for medium-weight English, and certain classes of shoulders share the same position.

Hides maintain a very firm position, and, taken all round, the balance of change is against the tanner • the latter has been disposed to ease off as much as possible with his purchases lately, and the brokers report the home trade slow, but the general position is entirely unaffected, as the Continent and America con- tinue to buy largely, and a continued appreciation is only too certain. SOLE LEATHER.—There was a very full demand for all classes of English, with a specially strong demand running for 12's and 14's. Heavies are coming steadily in dernand,and current production went out in full. There is a demand for the cheaper lines far in excess of the supply, and either branded or clear S.A.'s or Madagascars found an equally eager market. Shoulders grow in demand if not in supply, and conse- quently practically all classes other than extra heavies are dearer. Bellies were a quick sale all round. ROEGII LEATAER.- Straps moved rapidly, and for all weights under 241b.the demand is extraordinary. Rough belies and shoulders are in good demand at late rates.—From "The Leather World."

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

AGRICULTURE. As I read in my " Examiner " the Grappenball " par " respecting the County Council lectures on horticulture, I thought of an old acquaintance of mine, news of whose premature death in the United States had reached me only the day before. When he first came under my notice, be was errand boy at a Co-operative Store, and when, some half-dozen years ago, I had my last interview with him, he hid attained to a responsible position in an agricultural college in America. He had some wonderful stories to tell of the great work they were doing for the farmers in that region, and it seemed to me at the time that we were hopelessly behind i.i such matters. I have since had good reason to modify that opinion. Our County Councils are realising that agriculture is still our leading industry, and are making provision accmd- ingly. Excellent work is being done in that direction nowadays. NOTEWORTHY" FINDS."

The gruesome discovery at Lymm by men engaged in excavating in a new road reminded me bow one similarly employed once in my presence caught a fine specimen of a death's bead moth. Such things occasionally turn up in the most unlikely places. A while ago 1 was 'watching the unpa3king of a crate of bananas in a public market when a huge tarantula spider popped out and scurried away. It was quickly secured by a bystander, and is now in the local Museum. A member of my family was bringing coals to the kitchen fire when a newt was found alive and kicking on the shovel. It was the only one I ever saw in that district, and how it came there, and especially how it had survived its long detention in the coal cellar, was a mystery. KING'S PROJECTED VISIT.

The coming of His Majesty to Warrington was incidentally referred to at the Town Council meeting on the occasion of the Mayor's re-election. The hope was expressed that it would not be a " flying motor visit." As to that lam not very sanguine, Ido not think it would be wise to build one's hopes on the event I happened to be in a Yorkshire town on the day that the King pased through while touring the County of hroadacres, or rather the industrial sections. It had been arranged that lie should inspect one of the principal mills in the place, and as the Royal party came away I secured a good position on the route. The King and Queen, however, rode in a closed car, and motored by so rapidly that we caught but a glimpse of them. It was certainly disappcinting, but what would you? The object is to cover as much of industrial life as possible within a limited pericd, and if Warringtnn fares better than did communities in the heavy woollen districts of the West Riding I shall be agreeably surprised. THAT HAT.

In the course of the discussion to which I alluded just now, Alderman S.nethurst told how he had "lost one hat on the strength of one half of the Wariington Bridge Laing epened this year." I too, lost one about the same time. I was ero ;sing a cm tain b. idge when a sudden gust robbed me of my "tile" and dropped it in the liver. A kindly curate wh ) was passing at the moment thought to console me with : " Never mind ; come into my 'digs' with me, and I'll let you have one of mine." Ile meant well, but I'd "had some," and I thanked him and declined. At Blackpool last season, under the impulse of a mistaken idea as to comfort ,I invested in headgear of that kind ; but it gave me such a grave and reverend appearance that I missed lots of fun that must otherwise have come my way. Yet, after all,the situation was by no means devoid of humour. Thus, coming unexpectedly upon a lively group at the board- ing-house I heard one say to the others, "Shut up; here's that parson coming ! " I had other adventures ; but there is another hat story I must hasten to tell. In the days of the old School Boards, I used to attend th 3 meetings of one on which sat four Anglican clergy, a Roman Catholic priest, and a couple of Nonconformist ministers; and when they were not quarrelling about things that did not in the least matter, they were generally engaged in showing how frivolous and unbusinesslike they could be. The sick and weary reporters in attendance longed for a chance to expose them, and the opportunity came one night when the reverend father, tilting back the chair on which he had hung his hat, caused the " tile " to drop on the door. As luck would have it, it fell at the feet of the most athletic of the Church of England men, and be could not resist the temptation to use it as a football. Some of the others entered into the spirit of the thing, they let themselves go, and for a minute or two there was a scene surely unprecedented in the annals of public bodies. Participants and spectators alike enjoyed the fun for the moment; but next morning's discovery that the " lineage " scribes had made the most of it in the shape of " copy " for the dailies engendered altogether different feelings. The Board held a spocial meeting t consider the reporters' reprehensible behaviour ! After much wagging cf sobered heads and serious remonstrances withthe press representatives, it was formally and unanimously resolved that for ever thereafter the pencil-pushers should report only matters for the publication of which they had the Board's permission ! At the close of the proceedings, I ventured to ask the Chairman how it was proposed to enforce the resolution, the solemn adoption of which was even more farcical than the hat-kicking scene. THE BUSY MAN,

Councillor Pemberton is surpriscd that the Mayor, being a doctor, is able to give so much timo to the duties of his more public office. But it has often been remarked that "the busiest men have most leisure," and that there is a gcod deal of truth in the paradi x is easily demonstrable at a glance along the ranks of those who are in various ways voluntarily and appre- ciably serving their day and generation besides giving due attention to their own immediate concerns. From my own long list I take hap- hazard the case of a doctor who, besides following an extensive private practice, is school medical officer for a wide and populous district and poor law cfficer for one township, yet he cocducts ambulance classes, being an officer of the St John Brigade, readily undertakes lectures on a variety of subjects, and still seems to have plenty of time left for _amateur theatricals— his favourite recreation.

. ----- WELL PLAYED Last week's story as to " the difference a fly's leg may make "inevitably challenges comparison with the ancient wheeze respecting the stolid German musician. At rehearsal the irate conductor stopped the orchestra and demanded an explanation of an unfortunate foreigner who had made a bad slip— or appeared to have done so. " What do you mean by it ?" shouted he hotly; " there's no such note on your score. " More in sorrow for, than in anger at his chief, the impertubable German indicated the the " note " with his forefinger. " Why, 3ou fool," pursued the conductor with disgust; "that's a dead fly !" "No madder for dat," was the calm response ; "he vos dere, and I played him " ! ANOTHER SHOOK.

" A.H.'s story (ride "Warrington Noncon-; fortuity ") of the experience of the lady who was returning home from a ball brought to my mind one which I have been told concerning the late Rev. Peter Mackenzie. No doubt it is apeoryphal, but I will repeat it for whatever the reader may deem it to be worth. It is said that he had a daughter who was passionately fond of dancing. His remonstrances being in vain, he one day waylaid her on the stairs as she was going to her room after spending the night at a ball, and accosted her with " Good morning, thou child of the devil," and the girl simply and demurely replied—" Good morning, father !•' This in turn recalls one in which John Wesley scored after the like fashion. As he was strolling along the street, three young fellows burst upon him with the mock announcernent—" Mr. Wesley, the devil's dead." "Then," exclaimed the founder of Methodism, " whatever is to become of you poor fatherless children !"

The Examiner, Saturday, November 23, 1912.

SIR ROBERT BADEN•POWELL AND THE SCOUTS. A HIGHER AIM THAN SOLDERING.

A great meeting in support of the Boy Scout move- ment was held in the Macclesfield Drill Hall on Monday night. Sir Robert and Lady Baden-Powell were present, and the speakers included Sir George lteid and Colonel Sir E Cotton-Jodrell, who is the Scout Com- missioner for Cheshire. Mr Harold Whiston presided. —Lady Baden-Powell, who had a great welcome, was presented with a bouquet from the Girl Guides and a silver bag from the Boy Scouts. " • " -

Sir li-OE;ert Baden-POwell said ho believed that there were some people who imagined that there must be some form of military organisation, and that they were teaching the boys soldiering, lie was an old soldier himself and felt it a great honour to serve his country and his King, but ho knew that there was one step higher than being a soldier, and he was trying to get into it himself. That was to be a good citizen. That was the highest step of all, and that was the aim they were putting before the Scouts. It was the character of a nation that would bring it to the top.

In 1712, Thomas Emlyn had been fined a thousand pounds, and imprisoned until the debt should be paid, for a book which was deemed to be Unitarian. Keen controversy resulted and at the Salters Hall Conference held in 1719 it was revealed that quite half of the representative men who attended were unwilling to sign a declaration of belief in the Doctrine of the Trinity and of the Divinity of Christ.

WARRINGTON NONCONFORMITY. ITS 250th TINNIVERSTIRY.

THEOLOGICAL CHANGES. The Warrington Chapel becomes Unitarian. In recent chapters we hav'e sketched in briefest outline and with very insufficient detail the history of the Academy to the period when, weighted with misfortune, it closed its doors. It remains to say just a little about its influence upon the general trend of religious thought in England —an influence which was felt by those who taught as well as those who came to learn. Mr. Arthur Aikin Brodribb writing in "the Dictionary of National Biography" says of the Academy that " this Institution may be regarded as the cradle of Unitarianism. Though short lived it yet formed during the twenty-nine years of its existence the centre of the liberal politics and the literary taste of the County of Lancashire." There is truth in this, but we shall err if we take too seriously the statement that the Academy was the cradle of Unitarianism. As we have already hinted, the tutors who met in Priestley's day at the Saturday tea may have unconsciously influenced each other into new opinions, but Unitarian views did not originate at Warrington. More than half a century before the birth of the Academy, Socinianism had shown itself among both Nonconformists and teachers in the Established Church.and King William, as early as 1693, had received a deputation which asked him to forbid the

was made complete under the ministry of Dr Enfield. Of Mr Seddon it is said that he is "reputed to have been an Arian," and his teaching, doubless, prepared the way for the more pronounced views of his successor.

But the transition did not occur without internal strife, and it falls to us now to describe a secession from the congregation and the establishment of Independency in Warrington. It may, however, be most convenient to carry forward our account of the church by a mention of the ministers who have laboured there since the departure of Dr. Enfield. Dr. Enfield retired from his pastorate and Academy duties in 1785 to become minister of the Octagon Chapel at Norwich, and ended his days there after a dozen years of labour. He was succeeded by 'Joseph Bealey, who removed from Cockey Moor, near Bolton, in May 1786, and returned to his old church in 1791, after five years of ministry. His brief sojourn is memorable as having given to Warrington its first Sunday School, an institution of which we have already written fully in these columns.

gext in succession was William Broad- bent who took up his service in 1792 and continued for thirty years. He had been a tutor at the Dissenting Academies at Daventry and Northampton and seems to

===== 0002601_19121123_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
A PERIOD OF DRIFT.

We shall best understand the doctrinal position of the Nonconformists if we realise that the eighteenth century was a period of spiritual depression. Hume, the historian, declared that the people had " settled into the most cool indifference with regard to religious matters to be founa in any nation of the world. " Knowledge increased but not spiritual fervour. Many Calvinistic doctrines ceased to be credible, and in surrendering them many, even among the most enlightened, drifted strangely in their beliefs. Changes of doctrine of the most vital kind seemed to be regarded with com- parative unconcern. Processes of change were in rapid progress when Priestley began to exchange views with his fellow tutors at Warrington. His comment upon their opinion is :—" I have often tliought it not a little extraordinary that four persons who had no previous knowledge of each other should have been brought to unite in conducting such a scheme as this, and all be zealous necessarians as we were. We were Ilikewise all Arians, and the only subject of much importance on which we differed was respecting the doctrine of the atonement, concerning which Dr. Aikin held some obscure notions."

===== 0002601_19121123_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
DOCTRINAL MIXTURES

He considers it remarkable that they should be "zealous necessarians " because most of the Presbyterians had become Arminians. These four at least are still at one with Calvanism, and Priestley by his advocacy of the doctrine was earning the commendation of Toplady. This is very interesting in the light of Priestley's after opinions. Yet they were all Arians. _ _ _ _

Though- this is hardly the place for doctrinal exposition it may be explained that Arius was the Alexandrian theologian who was assailed as a heretic for teaching that Jesus was an originated being,and deny- ing that he was of the same substance as the Father. In the sixteenth century came Socinus, the Italian thinker, to give his name to another system of doctrine. Socinianism differs from Arianism in its denial of the pre-existence of Christ. It differs from Unitarianism in attributing to him a unique divine endow- ment, but admits of no plurality of persons in the Godhead, no original sin, no predesti- nation, no substitutionary Atonement. Though taking Scripture at its professed basis, it claims to hold nothing save what is rationally justifiable. Though Arianism is no longer taught in England, it had in the 18th century a period of influence. To many persons perplexed at the doctrine of the Trinity as it was then taught, Arianism seemed to offer a resting place between orthodoxy and Socinanism.Priestley, in writing of his Warrington days, says, 'The only Socinian in the neighbourhood was Mr. Seddon, of Manchester, and we all wondered at him."

• Mr. Seddon was probably the first minister in Lancashire to teach the mere humanity of Jesus. But he vas to have many successors. Priestley, who was an Arian in youth, sbecame a Socinian in his mature years, and carried with him vast numbers of thoughtful Nonconformists. Indeed, a large proportion of the Presbyterian Churches in England, led by their ministers, became Socinian and Unitarian.

THE CONGREGATION BECOMES UNITARtAN. The process of change occurred at War- rington as it was occurring in many parts of Lancashire. It began under Mr. Seddon, it

have sustained a helpful ministry. His labours closed with his death in December 1822.

The succeeding minister was Edward Robinson Dimmock who carried on the ministry until 1531. Following him for a period of three years Francis Bishop ministered, and after him Thomas Hincks for two years. Next came Dr. Philip Pearsall Carpenter, beloved of Warringtonians, and still held in fragrant remembrance. Of Dr. Carpenter and his work we hope to tell in some detail when leisure and space allow. Under his inspiring touch the quiet chapel became the scene of novel experiments and advocacies and his ministry proved singularly attractive. His period of service in Warrington extended to 1862. But not all the years were spent here,ia breakdown in health taking him for a short time to Montreal. It was after his return that the chapel was modernised and began to assume the cheerful and pleasant appearance which it wears to-day and which impresses every visitor.

A. M.

THE KNUTSFORD DIVISION. -- MR. P. M. OLIVER AND TORY DISORDER.

Speaking at a public meeting at Peover on Saturday Mr P M Oliver,the Liberal crrndidate for the Knutsford division, said the Conservatives had recently played a little game in the (louse, and they were 121 doubt delirious with jov. It was very nice to win, and to win a game • but this snap division was only a game and nothing more. The Conservatives bad made it inconvenient for the Liberal Party, but he thought that what had happened in the [louse of Commons that week would hurt the Con- servatives more than it would hurt the Liberal cause. Wear, hear.) It would make people realise that the Conservatives regarded politics as much more of a game than as real business. Mr Bonar Law asked for a free and full discussion, and this was agreed to. But was there frea and full discussion? Conservatives were allowed to speak, but Liberals were refused a hearing. The Opposition mad a a farce of it ; they turned the place into a bear-garden, or, as one of their own papers put it, into " a common pot-houss brawl." Now this was a most serious thing and he wanted his friends in that division t remember what had happened, for the Conservatives had for a long time called themselves the party of law and order. When books were hurled across the House and members lost their tempers he thought they ran the risk of

LOSING ALL CLAIM TO BE CALLED A PARTY OF

GENTLEMEN. They tried to ma'ie out that it wee net intended to strike anyone nhen the iriok was hurled, but that did not modify the violence of the thing. To hurl a book across the House of Commons in the direction of Ministers of the Crown was one of the most degrading things he could imagine. But what was it all about, this shouting and disorder ? It was but Home Rule. Be wanted them th remember just exactly what it was, because his Conservative friends got so hysterical about it, and people got wrong impressions. All they wished to do was to set up an Irish Parliament which would have control of home affairs. It would have no control over the army and navy or foreign policy. Surely there was nothing very revolutionary in that, and it should not be a thing to cause gentlemen in Parliament to hurl books about or lose their tempers. But why did the Conservatives lose their tempers? Simply because they knew that the Liberals were 113 W on tne same footing as they were. It was very good that rich men should give their services to the country whether in Parliament or on the Beech, but if we were to have representative government we must be able to make a free choice. WHERE RHUBARB CAME FROM.

Everybody is familiar with rhubarb and so perhaps everybody will like to know a little about it. It was first brought to England in the reign of Charles I. and was introduced through Sir Matthew Lister, the physician to the King. The doctor brought the seed from Italy and gave some to the naturalist, who managed to grow rhubarb. This would be about the year 1573 and it has been grown here ever since. But at first it was only grown because of the root. This was made into the medicine which boys and girls still have to take sometimes and over which they pull wry faces. It is only in recent times that the stalks were used for the making of jam and eaten as a fruit.

_ The rhubarb of our gardens is a native of Siberia and its name recalls its Russian origin. The ancient name of the river Volga was Rha, and as rhubarb grew upon its banks the name came to be applied to the plant. The Romans used it and called it Rha barbarum, which means the foreign root from the Rha. • The French borrowed the word when they began to use the, root and called it rhubarbe. We have borrowed the word and shortened it to rhubarb: DEAR LITTLE LABS. BY RALPH WELLES KEELER.

Dear little eyes, with blue eyes bright, Where did you come from through the night? What wonderland did you leave behind ? What have you come 0 earth to find? What road you came, whate'er You seek, You seem to have no word to speak. But your laughing eyes and your sunny smile Persuade me to bid you stay awhile. Yes, stay awhile and teach us here That with heart like yours we've naught to fear. And as we journey a long life through You sing to us and we'll talk to you. Well ask of earth what it has to give. - Together we'll study how best to live. And maybe, the land you left to roam, Will some day be our heavenly home. THIS WEEK'S STORY.

Here is a Scotch story told by Mr. Crockett.

It is a delightful example of Scotch humour. "A country laird with his man John was riding to market. The laird and John were passing a hole in the moor, when the laird turned his thumb over his shoulder and said, ' John, I saw a tod [fox] gang in there.' "'Did ye, indeed, laird ? ' cried John, all his hunting enthusiasm instantly on fire. Ride ye your lane to toon ; I'll howl 3 the craitur oot.

"Back went John for pick and spade, having first, of course, stopped the earth. The laird rode his way, and all day was fore- gathering with his cronies at the market- town, a business in which his henchman would ably, and very ably, have seconded him. "It was the hour of evening, and the laird rode home. He came to a mighty excava- tion on the hillside. The trench was both long and deep. Very tired and somewhat short-grained in temper, John was seated on a mound of earth, vast as the foundation of a fortress.

" ' There sna fox here, laird said John, wiping the honest sweat of endeavor from his brow.

"The laird was not put out. He was, indeed, exceedingly pleased with himself. 'Deed, John,' he responded,' I wad hae been muckle surprised gin there had been a fox in the hole. It's ton years since I saw the tod gang in there.'"

'A SEVERE BLOW."

LOCAL CLASS TEACHERS AND THE EVELYN-STREET APPOINTMENT,

The disappointment of local teachers at the selection of an outsider for the headmastership of Evelyn-street Council school was once more voiced on Saturday at the annual meeting of the Certificated Class Teachers' Association, held in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall. Mr Glover presided over a fair attendance. Sir G NEWBURN (secretary) presented his report. He said he regretted that there were still a few local teachers who had not joined the association. All were, however, willing to snare in the benefits of and ad van, tages fought for end gained by the workers. A heavy year's work lay before them, and it was only by united efforts that they could hope to obtain any measure of success. He much regretted, too, that the members this year received that severe blow which caused such intense dissatisfaction and disappointment. He referred to the Evelyn-street appointment in the boys' depart- ment. He wished to thank the committee of the association for the splendid work it did in gathering certain information from di ffarent educational areas, which had convinced many of the injustice which was done to local teachers. What was required was a regular scheme of promotion. (Hear, hear, and applause.) In foreshadowing the comiog year, he wished to draw their attention to the national petition for improved scales of salaries. A widespread effort was to be made to secure adequate remuneration for the men and women who were face to face with the fact that their whole professional life would be spent in the actual work of class teaching. bteps were being taken to secure a somewhat proportional representation of class teachers on the N.U.T. executive. He should like to see their association take active steps to augment or improve tee present scheme of medical inspection of school children. In conclusion, he seriously impressed on all present the necessity of making the association as atrong as possible. (Applause; The following officers were appointed :—President, Miss A Hitching ; vice-president, Mr E Cannell ; treasurer, Mr T Edden ; secretary, Mr G Newham ; committee, Messrs A Knowles, S Price, .1 Whitehouse, .1 Armstrong, F Longahaw, J Minards, and the Misses A Ewart, F Bolton and E Brookes. Miss KITCRING devoted her presidential address to the history of education in Warrington, beginning with the work of the monks of the Friary who were established in the town in the 15th century. She passed under review the foundation and progress of the Boteler Grammar Schoolehe Blue Coat Charity School, the Warrington Clergy lustitution, the Warrington Academy, the IVarrington Training College, the Mechanics' Institute, the School of Art and the Technical lustitute. In a report of 1881 on the extent to which Technical Instruction was already being given in Warrington, the following statement occurred : " At the Museum classes for German and French are being carried on end so far as we are aware that is the only instruction of any kind which is being given in public evening classes outside the School of Are" At a meeting of " The Warrington Association for the Promotion of Technical lestruction " h.3ld at the Museum, 21st November, 18s9, a discussion was opened by the delivery of an address " Wanted, an Institute," by Mr Madeley the librarian. The general indifference in the town as regarded technical instruction was commented upon, and after some discussion it was resolved "That immediate steps be taken to establish a Technical Institute in Warrington." As a result a technical institute was opened in 1891 and classes were held in Arpley-street School, School of Art, School of Science, Wilson Pattemstreet and in the Museum. The year 1889 saw the opening of the pupil teachers' day central classes. This system has in the last two years been replaced by the Bursar systsee under which the pupils studied full time for the first twelve months, and subsequently noted as teachers one day per week for the next twelve months. The present Municipal Technical Institute was opened in 1902 and a Secondary Day School begun. Then there were the Branch Tech,nical Schools for children who had left the Day Schools, To 011na DOW to Elementary Schools, the National School, Church-street, was opened in 1833. 1852 saw the opening of the Educational Society's School, or as they knew it, People's College, then situated in Newton. street. The founder was Mr Edward Brewtnall. Heath* side School was opened in 1854, and later on a new girls' department in It )llesten-street, was generously erected at the expense of Mr 13eamont. In connection with the Roman Catholic Schools, St. Alban's came first, the mission itself dating from 1771. The League 'of the Cross Hall, Bewsey-street, was used first temporarily, the present school being opened in 1863, whilst the present St. Mary's School was opened in 1871, and Our Lady's, Latchford, in 1872. There were now In Warrington 26 schools, with 41 departments, including the new Council Schools. By the Education Mt of 1870 Warrington still continued with voluntary contributions. A certain proportion was handed over to the managers to defray cost of keeping the building in repair. Consequently, when on Ist May, 1910, school fees were abolished, and that meant a great loss to some, appeals were made for special contributions. In 1861 the principle of payments by results was intro- duced, and from that time onward a marked mechanical improvement in the schools was purchased at the price of much that was hest in education. This system led to cram, and was a sure way to spoil teaching, weaken the interest of the pupil and misdirect the whole pur- pose of education. Inspection of methods had now replaced this principle, though the inspector might require from time to time to test the efficiency Of these methods. In the equipment of the schools many improvements had been made. The teaching of almost every subject had undergone a change, and it might be said was still changing. She thought they were in an experimental stage even now. But, after all, oue bait of the children in their schools came from such squalifl homes that their school days should be made as bright as possible, bearing in mind that the child of to-day was the citizen of to-morrow. (Applause). Miss Eitcbing was cordially thanked for her address; which evidenced considerable research.

• DIVORCE LAWS. Local Condemnation of Majority Report. Uncompromising criticism of the Nlajority Report of the Royal Commission on Divorce was forthcoming at the annual meeting of the Warrington Branch of the English Church Union, held in the Masonic Hall, Bold-street, on Saturday afternoon. The SECRETARY (Rev J H G Hollingworth) in pro- tenting his annual report, said the recommendations of the Divorce Commission would make no difference in the attitude of Claurchpeople whose duty it was to maintain the laws of the Church. The CHAIRMAN (Rev W J Stanton) said that, as citizens, they roust feel very grateful that there was a Minority Report, and also that that Report, chich aimed at not increasing further the fazilities for divorce had the very great weight of " The Times" behind it. In ceclesiasticsl matters they were very seldom in agreement with " The Times," but it was pleasing to find that on a question of morality that Lewspaper, in a very pronounced way, upheld the Minority Report. The Majority Report recommended that divorce should be granted for live additional causes. Of course, that was very distressing to them as citizens, because they I believed that marriage was indissolutde, They believed, as Christ ians, and as the Church had taught in the'West, that there was no dissolving of marriage during the lifetime of one of the partners and, since that was for a long time the law of England and divorce had only been amongst us a comparatively short period, it was naturally a very grievous to them that a further retro- grade step—or fi e further retrogade steps—should be taken. Their duty was perfectly plain. They were bound by the law of the Catholic Church as it had teen received in this re ilm. The report could not posibly make any difference to them as Churchpeople. Having contended that only separation could he perm ittechthe speaker said they ought to show now more plainly than over that they disapproved of divorce, whenever the question came up. They ought to be perfectly honest end show, as far as they could, in their social dealings, that they would not counten- ance divorce or regard it as respectable. There were many people whs, did not rare a pin about the opinions of the Church or, he was afraid, about religious opinions at all, but who did care very much about respectability and about the opinions of those around them. It was the duty of Churchpoople at this juncture to show very distiactly—as, to do them justice, he thought they had done in the past—that th.y did not consider divorce respectable.

THE NEW LIBERALISM,

INTERESTING ADDRESS TO THE JUNIOR LIBERALS.

INTERNATIONALISING POLITICS.

Militarism and Tariff " Reform."

"The concern for human betterment is the main spring of the new Liberal movement " declared Mr S R Dodds, M.A., of Liverpool, in a lecture on " The New Liberalism" before the members of the Warring- ton Junior Liberal Association in the new club rooms, Horsemarket-street, on Monday evening. Captain 11. GORDON ROBERTS, who presided over a good attendance, said his Liberalism was well known to all of them. Most of his friends—unfortunately, as he thought, for them—belonged to the Tory party and because he supported the policy of the present Government they were fond of dubbing him a Socialist. That he strongly disclaimed, for, if Socialism meant anything at all, it meant the stamp- ing out of individualism. Did the legislation of the past seven sears or the programme of the Liberal patty to-day in one single detail suggest the suppression of the individual ? He said emphatically "No." Then he was not a Socialist. The Liberal patty had passed into law s...smo of the finest social reform measures in the history of the world. Dis conception of the new Liberalism was that its sole aim and object was the guarding of the happiness and well-beirg of the people, linked up with equality of opportunity for all and privilege for none. (Applause.) Contrast that with Cunservatism, which had been always been the party to protect the privileges of the classes at the expense of the masses. (Applause). Mr Dorms first emphasised the fact that the new Liberalism was based upon the old. We still enjoyed the legacy of Mr Gladstono's times and several of the great questions which occupied the later days of the 'grand old man " were in tie forefront of politics to-6y. First there was reform of the House of Lords. Mr Oladstone's arguments agair st that House had been strengthened by the action of that body since his death. I All that he arid had been fully ju3titled. (Hear, hear). For all effective purposes, during the years 1895-1005 the House of Lords was as dead as a door nail. It exercised none of the functions of a Second Chamber but mechanically registered the decrees of the Tory 'party. In the 3 ear 1000 the Lords suddenly aA oke and found they had great functions which they must exercise most assiduously whenever Libe, al legislation was brought forward. The C3SO for the Parliament Act was overwhelming. (Applause.) Sir John Simon had summed it up in a senteLce :—" From 1000 to 1910, four full }ears, though wo had in the House of Commons the biggest Liberal majority in living memory there was not a single Government measure which the remnant of the Oppcsiti( n in the House of Commons thought lit to vote against on third reading which ever rgached the statute book." And so the Parliament Act was passed—a great measure though

A MODERATE AND A TEMPORARY ONE.

When tho more pressing Bills had berm dealt, with the Government would see the: th,dr promise to reform the House of Lords and place it upon a basis which wculd make it more useful to the government of the country, era fulfilled. Dealing with the first fruits of fhe Constitutional reform, Mr Dodds first alluded to the Insurance Act acd contended that the House of Lorda'failure to take advantage of their powers to delay it was an evidence of the insincerity of the Tory con- tention that the country had not been given sufficient time to discuss the measure. Referring to ten perubce, he submitted that the marked improvement which tad taken place in comparatively recent 3 ears, vvs to be credited not to govercmonts but to the unremitting work of the temperance societies. Mr. Balfour placed two I leen:ring Ac's on the statute hook hut they were not, of much use, because they were practically framed by the brewing interests. When a real temperance measure came before the House of Lords, it was doomed before it reached its second reading. With regard to Franchise Reform, there was hardly a man in the country who would stand up for plural voting, hut when a measure to abolish the system was sent up, the House of Lords nest it out. Totfching upon

WELSH DISESTABLISIIMENT, the speaker said the Welsh nation had waited long and patiently for justice. Never was rt country SO misrepre- sented as Wales was iu the House of Lords for, while the country was overwhelmingly Liberal, the Welsh bishops were to be found on the Conservative side of the House. Coupled as it was with disendowinent, the H,sestablishment Bill had caused henrt-burnings on the part of some people who were Liberal in temdency. In the hands of the Liberal Governmett, not only would justice be done but generosity shown in the manner in which the endowments were dealt with. If that generosity were not taken advantage of, the Con- servative party would have to be blamed by the Church party in Wales, because there never was a time whenthe Church party could get better terms than at the present day, if they would only show a little reason, and not "go for " their opponents as if they were aat of rascals and thieves. The cis° for Home Rule for Ireland had 13.. en strmgthened by the wonderful success which had attended the granting of self-government to South Africa and tha work of the Comity Councils in Ireland, notwithstanding Lord Salisbury's prediction that to grant County Councils to Ireland would be even more dangerous than Home Rule. The new Liberalism, con- tinued Mr Dodds, was mainly in the direction of economic and social reform. In place of constitutional and legal reforms, they were now concentrating their attention upon

IMPROVING- THE STATUS AND MATERIAL WELL-BEING OF THE WORKER.

They intended to give him better Conditions, in order that he might better himself. As Mr Winston Churchill had said : " There is a cry which i breaks upon our ears of intolerable suffering from a section, from a large proportion of our great population in this island. Civilization which has brought us much, science which gives so many things of use to a large proportion of our population can give nothing whatever to the poorest and weahest." The Right lion. John Morley had thus vividly referred to the lot of the toiler : I remember once talking to John Burns. I said to him : How does a man do when, with wife and children, owing to soma change in the money market or elsewhere the concern stops and ho is thrown out of employment and deost know which way to turn ' ? What does be do ? Perhaps he has got a little in a benefit society. If will tell you what it ia. An ordinary English toiler goes into his life like a man goes into battle. lie noes not know but that to-morrow be may get a bullet into his heart. The soldier does not know in battle ; the workman dces rot know whether to-morrow he may be cast into a ditch." Mr. J. M. Robertson, a leading Liberal doctrinaire, had thus put the case : "The vast majority of men are born to servitude, while a certain minority is, by reason of a simple hereditary positkn, born to a far easier life. I will suggest to any middle-class man who won't admit that, that be should imagine the case of his dying and leaving his femily unprovided for, and tbe possibility of his daughters having to go to work in a factory." These ills must not be remedied by class legisla- tion which only removed one grievance to create another, and the Liberal Party therefore conceived its policy with a xiew to the well-being of the community as a whole. Liberalism was therefore distinct from Socialism on the one hand and Conservatism on the other. It steed in the middle, " recognizing in the light of as mpaty and a sense of justice the indistructible rights of the people end troubling itself only with the degree of rapidity with which their demands can be m."

SOCIALISM.

Was a creed which deserved a great deal of considera- tion. Certain men were Sscalists from perfectly sincere convictions, but he maintained that Bocialiens would make too much for class legislation—it was the reaction from Conservatism, which had concerned itself with class legislation in the other direction. Mr DoclOs then passed under review the legislative record if the past few years and showed how all sections of the nation had been considered. He referred particularly to Old Age Pensions, the Housing Acts, Work- men's Compensation Act, National Insurance Act, .c.-:hop Hours Act, Children's Act, Minimum Wage Act, the Miners' Eight Hours Act and the Insurance Act, showing that the principle of sympathy lay behind all those measures. The Insurance Act, he said, had been on asset to the Conservative party during the last few months Lilt it would re-make Liberalism in the days that were to come. (Applause.) Although the Act might have defects, it was, in its main outlines, the most wonderful scheme of sickness insurance that the world bad ever known. !Applause.) He predicted that the day would come when the Conservatives would be only too ANXIOUS 10 WIPP: AWAY TIIN: SPEECJIES Of 1912. (hear, hear). The new legislation meaut the spending of much money under Covernment supervision but

taxation should not he complained of so long as it was for a useful purpose by which the community as a whole would gain. The speaker emphasised the necessity for taxation in proportion to the ability to bear it and specially commended the taxation of land values which, he said, the Conservatives would not dare to dis- continue. In the words of D C Brodrick, a writer of the aristocracy, "It is certainly impossible to ignore the grave political danger involved in the simple fact that nearly all the soil of Great Britain; the value of which is incalculable and progressively advancing, should belong to^a section of the population, relatively small and progressively dwindling." Touching upon the

_ _ LAND MONOPOLY in the centre of London, the speaker pointed oat that twelve landlords took £20,000,000 per annum in rent. That value bad been created by the community and Mr Lloyd George intended that such a system should not be perpetuated. (Applause.) had the taxation of land values been adopted a couple of generations ago, the nation would have reaped tremendously. We must decrease tho burden of unnecessary taxation and to do that our politics seeded internationalising. That solution would settle both the naval question and the Protection question. It was awful to think of the vast amount which was spent,without any compensation, upon the navy in time of peace. It was of course, necessary to keep a navy which would enable us to feel that our shores were perfectly safe, but why Ihonld it be necessary, at this stage of civilization, to have such an enormous navy in time of some ? It was because of tho anti-foreign prejudice that was nurtured amongst us, especially by the Tory party. It was signifLaut fact that OUR MILITARISTS HAD BECOME TO A LARGE EXTEN' IDENTICAL WITH THE TARIFF REFORM PARTY. Tariff reform was largely a" little England" movemenl —a movement which would dissociate us from the rest of Europe and increase the bitterness bet een different countries. It w:s a movement which required a tremendous navy to carry it out. The Socialists and the Labour party were to he highly commended, above all other parties, for the way in which they were link- in; up nation with nation The effect of that inter- national movement upon our English politics would be vast in the extreme. The question of naval policy would be settled upon commercial lines. NVe were becoming so Liter-looked in commerce that war would in time become impovsible for business reasons. Our success did not depend upon the defeat of another country. When another coun'ry lost, we lost toe,. The concern for human bettermer-t was the main spring of the new Liberal movement. (Applause). Air E Liovio JONES (chairman of the Association), in moving a vote of thanks to the Chairman and spealer, congratulated Orptaia Roberts on his appointment to the m&gisterial bench. Mr G TOULMIN (vice-chairman) seconded and said the Tory programme arp.ared pitif 1 in comparison with the work and aims of Liberalism.

The vote was lhartily accorded. The CHA IRM AN, in returning thanks, said he was not a little navy man ; he believed in having as strong n navy as possible. Ftee it do to us vvAs our bread and butter. We were the largest ship owners in the world and carried 70 or 80 per cent of the world's produce. In time of war those vessels had to be guarded by somebody and we lal to find the means of protecting them. Forturpitely our colonies were realizing their responsibilities in the matter and would help us mere as time went on. (Applause.)

MUSEUM COMMITTEE LECIURES.

"THE ROMAN WALL."

The departure made last year by the Museum Com- mittee in arranging a series of popular lectures wes to cordially welc,nned that it was decided to continue this branch of educational work this winter. The first of the series, a lecture on the " The Roman Wall, " was given by Mr J E Phythian on Saturday. To-night (Saturday) his subject will be, "The old castles of England end VN ales " and next Saturday be will lecture on Westminster Abbey." Limelight illustra- tions give an added charm to the lectures. There was a large attendance on Saturday evening in the. Masonic Hall, Bold-street,over which Mr T J Ridgway of Lyrnrn presided. In his opening ren.ari a the chairman referred to the increasing desire on the part of the public te listen to lectures on such eincational subjects. 'I ho Museum and Library Committee had rgain teen for tunal e in securing the assistance of the Ogden trustees for those lectures, The four lectures which were given last sear bad been so successful that this year a point was strained and it wes decided to have six, and all would deal with interesting subjects. Mr. PIIYTiIISN treated his subject in a lucid and interesting roatner. At the outset he said that the three lectures he would give were intended as a stimulus to patriotism. He did not think they would make one want to fight the Germans (Laughter.) They did not want that kind of patriotism but a patriotism that could exist if ours was the only country in the world, a patriotism that was not for one's self but for one's country. That was the true patriotism—not one's country in opposition to someone else's. If all the nations of Europe could agree that they could get on better when other people were doing so, it would be t 3 the universal advantage. It would, however, take them a long time to learn that lessor). Whet they did with their jealousies and rivalries was this : The German jealousy of us was preventing them doing their best for their own country, as they bad to spend money wastefully, and W 3 were also doing the same thing; but if we could only agree that we c Add get on better without that we should have more to spend on our own country and for the benefit of each other. (Hoar, hear.) There were no politics about that statement. We ought to love our own country, hut how could we do so without knowing it? Could we shut our eyes in imagination and see England? Could we picture its hills, valleys, beautiful country and its more than hidcons towns ? Did we know it in that secse ? We cold know it a great deal by pictures and illustrated hoots and so forth, but it was only by knowing it that we could love it properly and understand its problems. In Devonshire the people had not the slightest idea of what the great industrial district of Lancashire meant. They did not know what , life was here, and what our commercial undertakings and responsibilities were. When ho went among the people there he felt himself to be in an entirely different world, and when he talked to people there about what was taking plire in this part of the country be found that they had neither knowledge, imagination, nor a desire to now the needs of the vast population in this part of England. The political and economical notions down there were. simply absurd in relation to the great problems of this country as we understand them in Lancashire and Yorkshir. It required an effort to know our own country and oven our own town. He ventured to think that the three lectures he would give wore in a sense a contribution to patriotism—to the lova of one's country. (Applause) The lecturer then dealt with the early visits of the Romans to Britain and the, building of the Roman wall across from the Solway Firth, a distance of 73 miles. Unless the Romans came near a hill, the wall never turned to the right, or left, but when they did, the wall wes taken out of its course to get it over the top. Every four miles there was a small military town and every Roman mile there. Nem a little fort to hold a certain number of soldiers. Between each of the miles there was a sort of sentry box, and in case of danger the soldiers would be hurried up for defence. Slides depicting the wall were shown and the lecturer said they could not call the wall beautiful or architec- tural ; it was built for purely defensive purposes, and yet it appealed to the imagira ion. Another illustra- tion was that of a stone bridge, and when he saw .it with its masonry ho thought it was even more wonderful than the things one raw in Rome. Let them think what it was to got so far north. They had to cross uncivilized coumries, forests and marshes, right through Italy, over the Alps, right through Gaul, cross the English Channel, had to wait, for fair weather, as in those days there were no turbine steamers, land at Dover, as Julius Cesar did, had to work their way up the country, and when they came to the north they had to carry a bridge across the river. Such a vast work was a marvellous tribute to Roman power. (Hear, hear, and applause).
