WIRE WORKER'S DEATH.

Can Blood Poisoning Set Up Tubercular

Disease?

Can blood poieoning set up or accelerate tubercular clieeve ? was the interesting point riked in an applica- tion made by Thomas Hill, butcher, Winwick-road, at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Stinger, K.C., for compensation in respect of the loss of his son Thomas echo died on March 30th as the result, it was alleged, of an accident sustained whilst following his employment at the works of the Longford Wire Co. Ltd. on September 21st, 1911.1 Burgis, barrister (instructed by Mr T S Steel) I appeared for the applicant and Mr Metcalfe, of Bristol, represented the respondents. Mr. Borgis stated that the deccrsed vas a wire tester and had been employed at the Longford Wire Com- pany's works for some months prior to the accident. Ile began with wages of 158 per week and was ultimately raised to 18s. On September 21st, 1911, he went to work at six o'clock in the everiing and returned at six the following morning. An hour later he complained of pain in the thumb. His mother poulticed it, but on September 23rd the pain increased and Dr Pencocke; who was called in, diagnosed the case as one of blood-poisoning. Notice was given to the employers, who paid compensation at the rate of 108 a week. The young man's condition grew worse, and it was noticed that something was the matter with the hip. It %via; treated for tuberculosis, two operations heing performed, but the deceased died on March 30th. The immediate cause of death was hip disease, but it was contended that it was accelerated by the blood- poisoning which had the effect of reducing his vitality, thereby giving the tuberculosis gums full play. Some- thing like £7O was spent by the parents on medical t-eatn3ent and operations. The deceased used to con- tribute 12s a week to the family, and it was estimated that by his death they lost 4s a week profit. In cross-examination Mrs Hill maintained that her son gave her 12s a week regularly. She did not know that one week he had only earned 89 2d. If that were so, he must have had money saved up to pay her the lull amount.

Do you know that his average earnings only work out at 13s 100 for the year ?—No. And he clothed himself and had pooket money out o the rest ?—Yes.

Witness admitted that her husband was fairly well off and owned eighteen houses. She knew the certifi- cate of death gave as the cause" tubercular disease of the hip joint,' but it was through the blood poisoning. Her son was never medically attended before the accident. The applicant was also called and said his son had enjoyed excellent health bet, re the accident. Dr Peacocks stated that the condition of deceased's thumb was consistent with its having been cut by wire Mr Burgin Was there any connection between the tubercular hip disease and the blood poisoning ? • I consider that his vitality was lowered and consequently he was more liable to the hip disease starting. Supposiag there had been no blood poisoning, do you think it likely that there might have been no hip disease ?—lt is possible. It might not have developed. Mr Metcalfe : I ask whether it is possible that the hip disease which was the cause of his death was brought about by the scratch on his thumb is a big question to ask. I dare say, but I want an answer.—l cannot answer the question teraight. I %vitt answer it, if you like, in my own way. His Honour : What is your answer? Dr Peacocks : If there were no tubercle in his s 3 stem, it could not; but if there were, it is possible for the hip disease to be brought about indirectly by the lowered vitality after the blood poisoning. Further questioned, witness said at was at least six weeks before be and his colleague (Dr Bowden) in con- sultation decided that the hip was tubercular. Was it tubercular disease of any standing or not ? It started in the thigh and went into the hip joint. He had no sign of it when I first saw him. How long do you suggest the bacillus had been in the thigh ?—lt had been there all the time, lying dormant. It had not begun to work at all before the accident ? —There was no sign of it, Further cross-examined, witn-ris said a post-mortem examination was not held, lie at first reported the case to the police but, on consultation with Dr Bowden be was told it was not necessary and he therefore certified. He did not mention the accident on the certificate because he did not think it had directly any- thing to do with the cause of death. I put it to you that you ware not certain whether it was the cauEe of death. It might have been or it might not ?—Yes.

His do-ri.Our: It was a matter on which you had

81 1. formed an opinion, but it was not possible to '— certain about it ?—That is right. ceo It was not possible to toLl certain that it accelerated death I—l could not. swear to that. 14°' Evidence as given by a wage's clerk in the reollo dents' employ that the deceased's wages for tile months averaged only 133. 101 d. per week. Mr Metcalfe submitted that in view of the me-lc evidence, there was no case for him to answer. His Honour upheld this view and gave judgment f°l the respondents with costs.

"THE PHILOSOPHY OF PESSIMISM."

ttiong

On Tuesday evening last in the Congregati. Parlour, Cairo-street Chapel, the Rev Dr Mellor g°l a public lecture on "The Philosophy of Pessimism an. the Rubaiyat of Omar Khayyam." There we° remarkably good attendance. Dr Mellor urged tbst pe,isitnism WAS a necessary stage in the developme% of rt flection. Superficial optimism was of no value le life and a great hindrance to progress. The only Out optimism was that which had been born out ° pessimism. No mu can properly appreciate the gl°filf of the ideal until he has realized how utt,ei worthless and bad is all that is less than the Me,:it Contentment with the present is the most evil OD— the that can afflict any human soul. Anyone may Get it

Kidney complaint is no respecter of persons. It attacks rich and poor, young and old alike. It is one of the most treacherous of diseases because it comes on so quietly,— it often reaches an incurable stage before it is found out.

If, therefore, you know your mother or father to have had kidney disease, or if your work is a strain on the back or exposes you to all sorts of weather, examine yourself occasionally to make sure your kidneys are keeping all right. If there is a sediment in the water after it has stood 24 hours, or if you get pain in emptying the bladder the probability is that you need a kidney remedy. Being obliged to get up several times in the night also points to kidney and bladder weakness. Other symptoms are: gravel, dropsical swellings, back- ache, rheumatic pains, continual drowsiness, and sometimes dizzi- ness.

The beneficial effects of Doan's Back- ache Kidney Pills are often found after the first box or two—the bladder acts more freely and without pain, the water in dropsy is released, and the uric acid deposits in rheumatic patients are dis- posed of. Other cases are harder to treat because they have been neglected longer; but Doan's Pills have been successful in even advanced cases of dropsy, stone, lumbago, rheumatism, and inflammation of the kidneys and bladder. Doan's pills are pleasant to take, and are guaranteed free from any injurious ingredients whatever.

ST. HELENS.

ST HELENS LIBRARIES. BEECHAAI AND CARNEGIE GIFTS.

The thirty-fourth yearly report of the St Helens Public Libraries Committee, issued on Wednesday by Mr A Lancaster, contains a reproduction of Aldeiman Sir Joseph Beecham's coronation gift to the town—the large oil painting, " The Declaration of War," by Sir James D Linton. This is the ninth oil painting Sir Joseph has given to the public museum. The report describes as the chief event of the year the generous offer of Mr Andrew Carnegie to defray the £3,000 cost of a new branch library at Thatto Heath. The present library premises have become quite inadequate for the growing requirements of the district, but the new library, which will he erected in the park, will provide ample accommodation for books and readers, and will include a juvenile reading-room. This will be the third branch library provided for St Helens by Mr Carnegie, and brings the total of his gifts to £9,000. During the year the total Issues of books were 260,728, against 282,479 for the previous year. This decrease is owing to a variety of causes, the principal being the exceptionally flue weather of last year, the increased number of animated picture shows which are visited nightly by large crowds of people, and the publication of a considerable number of cheap editions of interesting and popular books.

DIRTY CHILDREN. SURGEON-GENERAL SHOCKED AT ST. HELENS Mr J B Leach presided at the yearly demonstration in connection with the St Helens Baud of Hope Union on Monday. He said ,bat during the past year seven new societies had joined the union, which brought the number of branches up to twenty-one. During the year the union had been increased by 689 new members, and in doing that four societies had succeeded in doubling the number of their members. - - -

The Rev A M Mitchell, vicar of Burtonwood, said be was sorry to say that there were at the present time no hopeful signs as to the diminution in the drink traffic. The incredse in the consumption of beer was due apparently to the increase in the drinking habits of women. That was a most serious matter, because they identified with women that great and wonderful tact of motherhood. If young married women indulged in alcoholic drinks, the children were almost bound to suffer. In his opinion there was no greater wrong that could be inflicted upon any human being than to bring a child into the world with en inferior birth. The right of every child who appeared in the world was to be well.born—to be born of healthy parents and free from tainted blood. They ought to have more thoughtful, more reverent, and more healthful marriage. Temperance reformers might perhaps increase their power by granting the Parlia- mentary franchise to women. The granting of that power had been a great blessing to New Zealand in that matter. Surgem-General Evatt, E.B, spoke of his forty years' experience as medical officer for the Army in India and other tropical climates and described the marvellous change for the better that bad taken place. In the Army almost 90 per cent, of the men were teetotalers. If these things could he done in the Army why should there be so much drunkenness in the civil population? General Evatt commented on the appear- ance of the young children in the room who formed the large majority of i he small attendance, and asked "Are these the children of St Helens ? Then I am astonished at St Helens to see them so badly cared for." He regretted that the teachers and parents of the children were not present, for to them he would have said most earnestly "For God's sake keep the children clean." He was shocked to see children come to a meeting like that so dirty audio badly cared for. He had not seen It anywhere else, and it was better for him to say that there than to go up to London and say it. If the con- dition of the homes of the people was improved there would be no need for the cry of conscription.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
LEEDS COUNCILLOR AND MILITARY METHODS FOR BOYS.

Sneaking at an anti-war meeting held in Leeds on Sunday night in connection with the local branch of the Independent Labour Party, Councillor D B Foster, in moving a resolution protesting against the tendency towards war which characterises the policies of the European Governments of to-day, and urging the workers of all classes to continue their efforts towards universal peace, said they must pay close attention to the schools. Beware of the boy scout, he said. There Is no more sinister influence in British life than the marshalling of our boys under military methods. ./ would urge the citizens of Leeds to cleanse their schools of the militarism that is damning the nation.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0134.txt | included=true | included =====
A LABOUR M.P. ON TORY "MEANNESS."

Mr F W Jowett, M.P. for West Bradford, speaking at the opening of the new rooms of the Bradford Trades Council on Saturday night, referred to the recent dis- turbances in Parliament, and said that if the Labour Party had been the .cause of such happenings every newspaper in the land would have been proclaiming the unfitness of the Labour men, and every newspaper placard would have screamed to make the people disgusted with the Labour party. Bat Tories, being fashionable persons, were not to be judged by the same standard. This kind of men honestly believed that it was their duty to govern, no matter who bad the majority. He could forgive an outburst of indignation but such a premeditated plan of unruly ragging as this was too mean for words. Men who would consent to hide in lobbies, &c., and vote on something they knew nothing about, just to spite the Government, were not fit to be members of Parliament. He hoped that the Prime Minister would do nothing in the nature of backing down because representative government was at stake.

LYMM.

REGISTRAR OF MARRIAGES.-Our readers will be pleased to learn that at last a resident registrar of marriages, in the person of Mr H T Mercer, has been appointed for Lymm. This desirable consul:nation has only been brought about by the persistent efforts of that much criticised body, the Lyn3m Free Church Council. Honour to whom honour is due.

THE HIBERNIANS.—A public meeting for Irishmen and women in connection with the Ancient Order or Hibermans was held in the Coffee Tavern, Booth's Hill, on Sunday morning, for the purpose of discussing the advisability of establishing a Branch of the Order at Lymm. Toe principal speakers were Bro. D Currie (County Treasurer, of Warrington), and Bro E D Crowley (County becretary of Manchester). There wa3 a capital attendance and a successful meeting ensued, with the result that it was decided to form a Lyinm Branch of the Order. Bro Spencer was appointed president, Bro Foy the treasurer, and Bro Whelan the secretary. FARMER'S TRAGIC DEATH —The inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of Mr B %Whitlow, of Moss Hall Farm, High Legh, who died under the tragic conditions reported in our last issue was held on Monday night. Ile cause of the accident was midis- , covered and the jury returned a verdict of" Accidental death." The funeral took place on Tuesday at the Rostherne Perish Church, where a large number of sympathetic people gathered at the service in which the Rev H D Surridge, vicar of High Legh, and the Rev C 1-1 Conybeare, vicar of Hostherne, were the officiating clergymen. The chief mourners were the widow, Mr J Whitlow (uncle), his brothers and sister. Twelve local farmers' sons performed the duties of bearers. Many wreaths and floral tributes were sent.

LECTURE AT OUGHTRINGTON.—On Monday night the Oughtringtou Mutual Improvement Society held an open meeting in the Oughtrington Schoolroom when about 'JO members and friends enjoyed an excellent lecture on the "Norwegian Pjorcts' by Mr Parrott, assistant master of the Manchester Grammar School- The lecturer, who had been to Norway on five once' sions, was thoroughly conversant with his subject and being an experienced photographer had secured an excellent collection oi views whilst on his visits; these he had converted into lantern slides and they were exhibited by limelight at the above lecture under the skilful manipulation of Mr J Webb, jun. Mr J Rad- cliffe (ex-president of the society) took the chair in the unavoidable absence of the President,Mr J D B Smith. A vote of thanks to the lecturer was proposed by Mr T H Jones, seconded by Mr T Nash, and carried with applause.

- THE CHURCH ARMY.—For the past few months the North Cneshire Church Army Van, under the charge of Captain Pickersgill and his able assistant, Cadet Christian, has been doing a good work in the Lymm and Ought ri ngton parishes. The visit was brought to a successtuf conclusion on Monday night when Captain Pickersgill gave a lecture in the Rush Green Mission room on the "Church Army." The lecture, which was illustrated by nearly two hundred lantern slides, was brim full of interest, many incidents of pathos and I humour being given. Judging by the crowded attend- ance, the many eulogistic remarks made, and the requests that the van would visit the district again, one can safely say that the officers have been popular and the visit a success. The van left on Tuesday for Hale, Barns and Ringway, the Parish of the Rev J Edwardes- Evans, M.A. late tiLadmaster of the Lymm Grammar School. CONCERT AT THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.—There was a good attendance, composed of patents, friends, past and present scholars, and the members of Mr T Nash's Young Men's Class, at the " Sing-Song " held in the Grammar School on Saturday night. The major portion of the programme was taken up by Mr. Papworth, whose comic songs and patter caused much merriment ; and by some excellent gramaphone selections kindly supplied by Mr Ashworth. Mr Papworth's items (including encores) were :—" The other department, please," "When Father was Mother for a Day," "We bad a most delightful evening," "To cheer him up and help him on his way," "The story of my wedding," and" Henry VIII." Other pleasing contributions were : A song at the piano by Miss Rothwell which was encored, pianoforte solo by Mr Fielding, violin solo by Miss Fanny Holt, piano- forte duet by the brothers J ones, and a song "October," by the scholars. The accompaniments were tastefully provided by Miss D R Shaw. A vote of thanks to Messrs Parovorth and Ashworth was proposed by Mr C H S Willson, seconded by Mr Jones, and carried in the usual hearty manner.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0138.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE SLAVE BILL.

Two Meetings.

As the result of a conversation between a few prominent Lymm citizens a meeting dealing with the Criminal Law Amendment Bill and Contingent Questions was held in the Parochial schools on Monday evening. The Rev G B Thurston, M.A., who presided over a capital attendance, referred to the Bill in very sympathetic terms. Mr .1 D B Smith, the organizer of the meeting, read letters of apology for absence from the Rev F W Cooper, M.A., of Prestwich, the Rev R W B Whiteway, of Lymm,and Mr A Crompton,of Heatley. An able and convincing address was given by the Rev Kirk Maconnachie, of Manchester (secretary of the Anti-State Recognition of Vice). After outlining the movement which led up to the introduction of the pre- sent Bill, the speaker dwelt on the importance of the better class people of the cummunity interesting them- selves in the measure for not only were the poor people affected by it but many members of their own class. He said that whilst they all regretted the necessity of Including flogging powers in the Bill, yet he thought it was a justifiable mode of punishment for all offenders. In regards to the fear of wrongful arrest that some people thought might occur under the Bill he main- tained that there was very little likelihood of such a thing happening,for at the present time the police have certain similar powers and yet no trouble or mistakes are experienced, and even if a person was arrested on suspicion be was sure that their good characters would clear them. He said that the need of the Bill was for the protection of young women and proposed a resolu- tion in support of the Bill. In seconding the proposition Mr J D B Smith spoke of two objections that had been raised in Lymm against the holding of such a meeting as the present one. , first, it had been said that a resolution from an I unimportant place like Lymm would carry no weight with it, but he said that their resolution added to the others that were bling sent from all over the country would, he believed, convince the House of Lords of the large support that was behind the Bill. Second, it was asserted that the subject was greatly exaggerated but Mr Smith said that it was not the case and mentioned several cases in which the officers of the Vigilent Society had been able to prevent young women being taken from the country for immoral purposes. The resolution was carried unanimously.—On the motion of Mr .J H Holt, seconded by Mr G L Welford, a vote of thanks was accorded to the speaker and chairman.

===== 0002601_19121123_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN LIBERALS DISCUSS THE QUESTION. "The White Slave traffic is a highly organised trade with agents in every big city." Such was the remark made by Miss Cherry of New Brighton who delivered an interesting address on "The White Slave Traffic" to a large assembly of Women Liberals in the Primi- tive Methodist Schoolroom, Lymm on Thursday even- ing. The meeting was held under the auspices of the Lymm and District Women's Liberal Association and Mrs. Dodds of Penketh, presided. Mrs Wyatt (secre- tary of the branch) was also on the platform. Mrs Dodds briefly introduced the speaker and said she was sure they would all be delighted to hear an address on a subject which was of great importance to women. (Hear, hear.) Miss Cherry said there was nothing they had to be more thankful for at the present day than the fact that women were taking a greater interest in the evils which beset the nation. They had to put aside their prudishness and mock modesty and deal with the evils face to face. They wanted to bring about not merely political reform in the matter but a better moral condition throughout the world. She did not think there was so much indifference with the subject as ignorance of it and the Women's Liberal Association and kit dred societys were doing a good work in ender vouring to enlighten' the people. (Hear, hear). To the procurers the White Slave Traffic was a profitable business and London was the clearing house for the world. The " trade " was the brisker in America. It was highly organised with agents in every big city. Speaking on the question of the police having the power to arrest on suspicion, Miss Cherry said they would then be able to deal with cases that they could not touch before. In March last everyone had been sur- prised at the Government suddenly tatting the matter up and the papers asked" Why this sudden gash of virtue on the part of the Government ?" One paper s.id it was due to the Women's Liberal Associations for the pressure they had brought to bear on the Government (Applause.) A great deal was also due to the activities of the British Women's Temperance Association. The women who were working the hardest however, were the Suffrage Societies because at the bottom of that movement was that great moral ques- tion. After giving many facts concerning the traffic, the speaker said it was the duty of the parents to educate their children so that the blot upon the nation might be removed. (Hear, hear.) If England was to retain her position as premier nation of the world she could never do it unless she was cleansed, for it was righteousness that exalteth a nation. (Applause.) Mrs. Dodds said they were deeply indebted to Miss Cherry for the able address she had given them. (Hear, hear.) She could quite agree that ignorance was really at the root of the whole matter, and she felt sure that when women got to know all about the traffic and its terrors, they would extend their activities in the right direction. One never seemed to realise, in sheltered homes where boys and girls were well cared for, that Fuch a foul stream was running through our cities. She felt sure that women only wanted to know about the subject to have their indignation roused, and when it was roused they would soon set to work to remedy the great evil. It was a woman's question without a doubt,' and one bad to put away the natural shrinking from I speaking of such a question, and try to bring about a better state of affairs for the well-being of the country. There was a new spirit coming for the women of the country. They realized that there was something more to be done than sitting on a cmhion and doing fancysening workand so forth She felt that there were certain things which God had particularly gifted women to do and surely to get rid of this evil was one of them—(hear, hear)—to help those poor fallen creatures, and not only that but when they bad helped them to extend to them the right hand of fellowship. She felt sure some of them would go away from that meeting feeling that they were very ignorant and they would seek to know all they could to help in the cause and bring to bear on it their good commonsense. They wanted to look at the question 'from a commonsense point of view, not merely to be carried away by the excitement of a meeting. There was a cal to every woman who was worthy of the name of woman to do all she could to help in the great cause. She was quite sure the Liberal Women of Lymm would rouse up and do what they could to let their voices be heard against that terrible traffic. (Applause.) She then proposed the following resolution' That this meeting of the Lymm and District Women's Liberal Association cordially thank the Government and members of the House of Commons for having passed the Criminal Law Amendment Bill and respectfully urges that it be placed on the Statute Book as early as possible. Mr J U B Smith seconded the resolution which was carried unanimously. Refreshments were served and Mr Arthur Leyland greatly pleased with his singing to the accompaniments of Mr J Webb. LYMM PETTY SESSIONS. At. the Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednesnav the magistrates had a very busy time sitting from 9.30 a.m. to 2-45 p.m. The following were the cases :—John Swains was summoned for allowing his pony to strsy on the highway in the Station road, Partington, on the 15th of October. He was fined 2s. Gd.—James Newton was charged with riding a bycycle and when overtaking a foot passenger failed to sound a bell, whistle, or otherwise give audible and sufficient warning of approach in Warrington Road, Lymm, on the 13th of October 1912. He was tined ss. and 14s. 6d. costs.— Mrs Mary Walker applied for a music and dancing license at the Fox Tavern, Booth's B ill, Lymm, on the occasion of November 2iith. 1912, but was refused. PERMITTING DRUNKENNESS. 1 -- Landlord of the "Jolley Thrasher " Fined. There was an interesting licensing prosecution t Lymm Petty Sessions on Wednetday, when Herbert Munro, of the Jolly Thrasher Inn, Broomedge, Lymm was summoned for permitting drunkenness on licensed premises. Mr Caldecutt, solicitor of Knutsford, prosecuted for the police. Defendant, who was repre- sented by Mr Elision (Manchester) pleaded not guilty. Mr Caldecutt explained that defendant was not actually the landlord of the inn, but had been in charge for some weeks and had made an application for the transfer of the licence. On Saturday, October 12th at 11.45 p.m. P.C. Needham found a rnan named Norbury io a drunken condition in the yard of " The Jolly Thrasher" and called defendant's atten- tion to the matter. Mr Munro said "He has not been served here. He came in about 9.30 p.m., and started shouting. I then put him out and have not seen him until now." That was an important statement and he would call evidence which differed from it. The officer got the man uff the premises and he was eventually locked up for his own safely. Norbury subsequently made a statement to the police in which he said he had been in the Jolley Thrasher in the afternoon ; had also been to other houses, but that he returned to this house about 8.30 p.m. Then he left the inn, returning about 8.45 or 9 o'clock, and remained there the whole of the evening, and did not remember going out of the house. He would tell them he had some four pints of beer, one of which was served by the landlord. A man named Gibbon would tell them that he was in this house some time before Norbury was taken out. Norbury was told to go out by tbe landlord, but did not do so and went into the taproom ; that he came back again and the landlord attempted to put him out, and that he fell in the lobby and there was something of a struggle, and that the landlord and Gibbon carried him out and put him in the yard. Evidence in support a the prosecution's case was given by P.C. Needham, Sergeant Washington, William Norbury, teamsman,Broomedge, William Moss, William Yearaley, James Yearsley and Samuel Gibbon; the last bur spoke to seeing Norbnry at the Jolly Thrasher. For the defence Mr Hislop argued that the landlord did not known of the condition of Norbury, or his presence on the premises at 11.45. He disputed the testimony of some of the witnesses, and said that defendant refused to serve Norbury at 9.45 and put him out. There was no witness to say NorburY Was lin the inn after 10.15. The man made his way into the inn yard to sleep, and defendant was unaware of his presence. He had certainly not permitted drunkenness. Defendant bore out this statement in evidence. After putting Norbury out and refusing to serve him at 9.45, he saw him no more till the police drew his attention to the man. Further evidence was given by Miss Peck and Miss Taylor, the barmaids, George Davenport (who said he saw defendant put Norbury through the wicket gate into the lane), John Scott (who said he met Norbury, and passed him in the lane and exchanged greetings, at 9-40). Other witnesses were also called in support of the defendant's story. The Bench retired to consider their decision, and on their return The Chairman said the magistrates bad decided there oust be a conviction. Defendant, who r ily recently had come to the house, came with good teatimonials, and if they could have seen their way to take a more lenient view of the case they would have done so; but Norbury was drun. got his drink on the premises, no reasonable steps seemed to have been taken to guard against it. There would be a conviction and the fine would be 20s and costs (including an advocate's fee of two guineas). The fine and costs in all amounted to £6 6s.

Confessing to a burglary committed about seven years ago in Farridgdon-road, Michael McCarthy, 25, a carman, gave himself up to the police. It bad been preying on his mind, he said, for a long time. He stole goods worth, he thought, about £2OO, but he bad given them for sale to a man whom he bad not seen since. McCarthy was remanded at Clerkenwell Police Court.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr J Spruce presiding.

KITCHEN RANGES, FIRE GRATES, TILES,

THE LATE MR. MC INTYRE.

His worship the Mayor (Alderman F. Neil) held his Mayoral procession to church a week later than usual. Sunday morning was cold and showery, but there was a go l crowd of sightseers. The procession, which started from the Town Hall-square at half-past ten for St. Paul's, was not of the dimensions of previons years. The bands of Messrs Gossage. Ltd., and the St. Helens Engineers headed the procession, followed by the Mayor, members of the Corporation, and officials and representatives of other local bodies. The fire brigade also mustered in strong force.

The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Rum]. District Council was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr C E Linaker presiding. .

THE LATE MR. MC INTYRE. On the motion of the Chairman seconded by Mr Antwiss a vote of condolence was passed with Mr W Mclntyre andfiamily intheir bereavment. The Chairman endorsed all that had been said in the Board of Guardians. In addition to all the qualities which had been mentioned—urbanity, geniality, affability and general business-like ability—Mr Mclntyre was a man with plenty of backbone and possessed more than an ordinary share cf common-sense. He always looked at things as they were and not as he wished them to be. The term "A grand old man "could be suitably and appropriately applied to him. Mr W Mclntyre briefly returned thanks.

URBAN COUNCIL'S " NON-POSSUMCS ATTITUDE." The Castner Kellner Alkali Co., wrote calling atten- tion to the fact that the question of who was to deal with the top end of Sandy-lane and bow it was to be put into a proper state of repair was still in the balance Temporary repairs had, they knew, been esecuted and they had contributed something towards them, but the position seemed unsatisfactory, apparently due to the non-possumus attitu3e of the Urban Council. As the Rural Council were desirous of seeing the thing through and to do what they considered their duty in putting the lane :n proper order, it had occurred to them that if the Urban Council had such a poor opinion of the top end of Sandy-lane, they might be willing to consider an adjustment of the boundaries which would place the whole of this lane in the Rural boundary. They made this suggestion for what it was worth, but they did beg that the matter should not be allowed to drop into forgetfulness because it was very important that the traffic for workmen to and from these works, together with the road traffic of other descriptions, should have proper and adequate ways open for it. They asked that the matter should be given urgency and steps taken to complete this connection which was the only important one between Weston Point and Runcorn.

The Chairman pointed out that they had spent a great deal of money on getting the foundation of the road. The Runcorn Council ought to meet them in the matter.

The Clerk said the road was originally a waggon track.

Mr G Pilling moved that a letter be sent to the Runcorn Urban Council.

The Chairman said they had gone over their boundary with the road now.

It was unanimously decided that the Clerk write to the Runcorn Council on the matter.

..... On the recommendation of the Housing and Town Planning Committee, it was decided that the Frodsham Lordship Parochial Committee be written to in respect of sewerage matters, and also to take into consideration the carrying out ot sewerage in Netherton area.

Prior to the commencement of the ordinary business reference was made to the death of Mr J Mclntyre, an old member of the Board, and a vote of condolence was passed with Mr W Mclntyre and family in their breave- ment.

The Chairman referred to the deceased gentleman as a former member of the Board and also as chairman of the Board under the old conditions for a good many years. He was an excellent and highly respected member and be had a knowledge of the duties of a Guardian second to none. (Hear, near.) He moved the vote of con- dolence.

Mr Woodward seconded and said he sat under the, presidency of the late Mr Mclntyre and he could endorse all that had been said by the Chairman. He was a man whom to know was to respect, cons cien teoos in the performance of his duty and sympathetic to those. to whom he had ass Guardian to administer. He was always urbane and friendly with every member of the Board. He himself never hoped to sit under a more urbane, kindly, genial and businesslike chairman than the deceased gentleman, in the ordinary course of nature he was getting an old man but it would be better for the whole community if there were more men, of Mr Mclntyre's stamp and calibre. Mr. T Wright supported, and Mr W Mclntyre, in thanking the Board, said his father was a kind and good parent and always taught them to be straightforward itt everything, to look kindly on all around them and also to respect all those with whom they came in con- contact and especially old age. His death was a very great loss indeed, but at the same time it was some little consolation to them to know that he was so universally respected and to hear the, expressions from members of the Guardians. He took a great interest in the movements of the Board and also ,in its members. He was asseciated with the Board for 40 years and he never relaxed the interest in its work, but maintained it to his dying day.

PENSIONERS .AND RELIEF.

The Guardians of the Holborn Union forwarded copy of correspondence which they had had with the Board of Customs and Excise and the Local Government Board in regard to pensioners who are admitted to Poor Law Institutions for the purpose of receiving medical relief. The Guardians were of the opinion that the Old Age. Pensions Act should be amended so as to secure to a pensioner entering a poor law institution for medicaL tieatment, the right to cnntinue to receive his pension on the first four Fridays immediately following his admission, after which the pension, so long as the pensioner remains an inmate of a Poor Law institution,. either should be payable to the Guardians or cease. The Clerk said the only way in which they could "recover" was to obtain from the person in receipt or relief any money be might have in his possession by means of a magisterial order. As far as the pension was concerned, so long as they were entitled to receive it, they could do so themselves or give authority for someone else to receive it.

Mr Wright: I-in-o-ve we support it. Mr Davies : I seoond it. . _ _

The Clerk : They do not send a resolution. There is no question of supporting, but as to what action you will take, if any. Mr Morris objected to the word " cease " as it was in his opinion a kind of dog in the manger policy. Mr Linaker : There is a resolution before the ueeting. Mr Linaker, jun. : I don't think there is a resolution.. Mr Morris: I move we go on to the next business. After further discussion Mr Wright said he would withdraw his motion and it was then decided by the Council that no action be taken in the matter.

GRAPPENHALL.

LECTURE ON HORTICULTURE —Mr R GJames (county councillor lecturer,) continued his series of subjects on Monday evening last in the Council schools. The Rector, Rev H V Pigot, presided over a well attended meeting. The subject was "The propagation, pruning, and management of bush fruits." The lecturer dealt with practically every phase of the sabject in a very able and masterly way. His blackboard illustrations of land cultivation, and his practial demonstrations of pruning created much interest, and many new and valuable points were readily appreciated by the audience. This was verified by the numerous questions asked.

PADGATE.

MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY,—At the Wesleyan Church anniversary services were held on Sunday and Monday, on behalf of the Missionary Society of the Connexion. On Sunday the preachers were the Revs W A Beckett and E Morgan. A special missionary meeting was held in the schoolroom in the afternoon when short addresses were given to the scholars by Messrs Wheatley and Birchall. On Monday evening the annual missionary meeting was held in the schoolroom. Mr William Bennett presided over a good attendance and spoke of his lifelong connection with, and interest in missionary work. The annual report was read by the Rev E Morgan, who later commented favourably on the increased collections for the second year in succession in connection with the anniversary. The special deputation was the Rev TH Weinman, who for many years was a missionary in South Africa, and acted as a Wesleyan Chaplain during the Boer War. The rev, gentleman kept the audience spellbound by dramatic descriptions of his adventurous career. A special feature of the meeting was a living map of South Africa. A number of Sunday school scholars were grouped upon the platform, being lines representing Cape Colony, Natal, Transvaal and Orange River Colony. Each section in turn held aloft a card bearing its name and sang a verse of a hymn, the Rev T H Weinman explain- ing the idea to those assembled. At the conclusion of the meeting a vote of thanks to chairman and speaker was moved by Mr Arthur Bennett, seconded by Mr T B Duck worth and heartily endorsed by the audience. The Bev T H Wainmain replied in suitable terms.

Sr. MARY'S INFANTS SUNDAY SCHOOL.—On Tuesday evening a successful social was held in the above klehool to mark the event of opening the school after the alterations and additions. The work which.'as been done includes two extra class rooms and ladies and gentlemen's lavatories. The work has cost about £l5O. The social commenced about 7.30 and was opened by a few suitable remarks by the Vicar, Rev J R Jones. A dance, whist drive and games were entered into with great zest. LABOUR PARTY'S NEW CLUB.—On Saturday evening last an interesting event took place at the Penketh Institute in connection with the Penketh Labour Party. The party hs for some time felt the need of a p ace to meet for social and political purposes especially for the younger members of the party, and the Institute at Stocks-lane was opened as a club, with a potato pie supper and social. The social commenced about 7.30 p.m. and the company numbering about 80 sat down to supper at 7.45 and all did full justice to the repast. The committee were fortunate in arranging for Mr Speakman (Earlestown), the chairman of the Newton Division Labour Party, to be their principal guest on such an interesting occasion, Mr. Speakman, who is devoted to the causes of the Labour Party, and counts nothing too much trouble which is in any way forwarding the party's interest, said he was delighted to be with them that evening, and wished them every success in their new ventures. The party is energetically supported by the local Women's Labour League, and owes a great deal to them for their efficient support.

The Ontario Government have arranged to transfer for the winter months their emigration officer, who bas been stationed at Quebec during the snmmer. Until the spring, he will be stated at Halifax, where many steamers disembark their passengers during the winter Heretofore, DO officer representing the province has been stationed at Halifax, but the great increase in emigration, even in the winter, has necessitated this step in order that settlers proceeding to Ontario may have the advantage of Government advice as soon as they land.

ETY REPO

Sir,— In your issue of 16th mat, the report of the Parliamentary Debating Society contained an unfortu- nate error, in the name of one of the speakers • on the Socialist amendment to the Insurance Bill. The name of Mr J Chamberlain was given as the member for Colne Valley, instead of Mr J Helsby. As Mr Chamberlain was at the bedside of his dying son on the night of the debate, your report would give pain to those relatives and friends who knew the true circurnstances,—Yours truly, 418, Knutsford-road, November 18th, 1912.

W. WATSON,

SOCIALISM AT CAIRO-STREET CHAPEL. Sir,—The special sermons recently delivered by Dr. Mellor, of which a summary has been published in the " Examiner" each week, have roused great interest amongst the Socialists in the town. A meeting held after the service in the chapel parlour on Sunday last was well attended, especially by Socialists. Many suggestions were made by them as to how best to spread Socialist:teaching. The workshop seemed by general consent to be a very favourable ground for sowing social discontent. One or two of the speakers sagaciously pointed out tbat care was needed as some people, especially employers, did not favour this particular method. It has many points in its favour. For instance in discussing and making converts during working hours much of the employer's time is occupied and as he was responsible for the wage, the agitation was carried on at his expense. This is, as it were "turning the guns of the enemy upon themselves." Furthermore it is very noticeable that when a young workman was con- verted to Socialism he became s enthusiastic that his capacity as a workman suffered. This again gives the employer workmen who regard the question of Socialism as of greater importance than anything else. These methods have the additional recommendation that they cost the Socialist notuing but ingenious arguments. Another excellent device is the securing of existing institutions for propaganda work. The Cairo-street Chapel is an excellent institution of honourable tradition one which the Socialists might press into the service for the spread of socialistic knowledge. The pastor is very active in promoting a healthy discontent with everything in general and it only remains for the Socialist to come forward with his programme. Many other institutions might be used in a similar manner. Look at trade unions. These have been quite captured by them. In some cases trust deeds might stand in the way of too strong an advocacy of political causes in places of worship ; on the other hand one may go far before opposition is encountered.—Youi s truly, Warrington, A COMRADE. November 22nd, 1912.

THE RIGHTS OF ANIMALS.

Sir,—Dr.-Ran—s-oil—fin—ds it convenient to ianore the fact that dogs, cats, horses and monkeys are used in vivisection, and concentrates attention upon "the humble guinea-pig." Well, even guinea-pigs have their feelings—and their rights. Moreover, there are differences of opinion as to the utility of testing milk on guinea-pigs. The milk of a cow obviously ill would, of course, be injurious to health ; and the remedy seems to be a stricter inspection of dairies. lilk from a healthy cow accused of harbouring the tubercle bacillus is probably as little likely to cause consump- tien as a healthy person alleged to be a "germ carrier" is likely to spread a fever from which he has never suffered. Thc•re are, of course, cranks who assert both these things, for the bacteriologist has for some time past diligently sought to drive commonsense out of the field, and in some quarters with considerable success. To wait from three to five weeks for a guinea-pig to develop con- sumption after inoculation would seem, outside laboratory circles, a dilatory manner of deciding y,hether we are drinking dangerous milk, to say nothing of the fact that Koch and his followers deny the possibility of infection through milk in any case. We may leave this question to be fought out by the rival schools, but in support of Koch's theory we have the fact that the children who most readily develop consumption are those who hardly ever get any milk at all. Dr. Manson is perfectly right in asserting that " if the anti-vivisectionists had their way this work would be further hampered and possibly abolished." We consider that the bacteriologist has run his fads at the public expense quite long enough, and that the only people who benefit by his activities are the manufac- turing chemists. Dr. Manson places experiments on animals in such a rosy light that it is necessary to remind him that the Royal Commission has stated that some of these experiments are productive of great pain and much suffering." We fail to see any profit in the excuse that other animals may benefit by this individual pain and suffering, and we certainly cannot compare it with the quick death required in slaughtering, still less with the labour which is required of certain animals, as it is requited of the mass of our own race. Even •sport " aims at quick death, although killing for pleasure is quite indefensible from a humane standpoint Dr. Manson should acquaint himself with the medical arguments on our side. He will not find them in the naive little leaflets of the Research Defence Society, although he will find that his own side is given equal publicity with ours in our literature, as the controversies containing both sides can be obtained on application to Mrs Holmes, Barnacre, Latchford Without, Warrington.—Yours faithfully, BEATRICE E KIDD. Secretary British Union for Abolition of Vivisection, Nov. 18th, 1912. 32, Charing Cross. S.W.

Sir,— Adverting to the letter of "J. Manson" in your last issue, may I suggest that the least of all possible rights of an animal is to be spared the worst of all possible wrongs, viz.: Vivisection. It is misleading in your correspondent to suggest that innumerable sentient creatures must be tortured to death to secure innocuous milk. When the latter is brought to the boiling point it is freed from any effective germ life and the food value moreover is not impaired. The last Parliamentary return issued July, 1912, gives the number of experiments on animals in 1911 as 05,203. The licences and certificates are given nominally by the Home Secretary, but practically by "the Association for the Advancement of Medicine by Research " which is composed of vivisectors and their friends who thus give the certificates to one another, the Home Office merely endorsing them. In 1911 licences and certificates were thus given to 562 persons. There were 42 registered places in England and Scotland in some of which there were several laboratories. Two Inspectors give part only of their time to visiting this mass of artificially created misery. There is thus another side, as we see, to J Manson 's story. As to the utility of vivisection, Sir Frederick Treves, surgeon in ordinary to the late King, is reported in the " British Medical Journal" as saying: "Many years ago I carried out on the Continent sundry operatiovs upon the intestines of dogs, but such are the differences between the human and the canine bowel, that when I came to operate on man, I found I was much hampered by my new experience, that 1 had everything to unlearn and that my experi- ments had done little but unfit me to deal with the human intestine." In like manner rirofessor Levi son Tait, one of the most ,eminent of English surgeons, said: "The conclusion of our experiments upon animals was that they could not be applied to human beings and the whole thing was a failure. These experiments (arresting hemorrhage from arteries) kept us back twelve years." Sir William Fergusson, F.R.S., sergeant-surgeon to the late Queen Victoria, told the Royal Commission, 1876: "I am not aware of any of these experiments on the lower animals having led to the mitigation of pain or to improvement as regards surgical details." The Government appointed Dr. George Wilson to be one of the last Royal Commission on Vivisection and Dr. Wilson writes as follows : "I accuse my profession of misleading the public as to the cruelties and horrors which are perpetrated on animal life. When it is stated that the actual pain involved in these experiments is commonly of the most trifling description, there is a suppressio yeti of the most palpable kind, which could only be accounted for at the time by ignorance of the actual facts." Should any of your readers desire to know the truth about the immoral and useless practice of Vivisection, a postcard addressed to our Hon. Secretary, Mrs. Holmes, Barnacre, Latchford Without, Warrington will bring a supply of reliable literature free of charge.—Yours faithfully, R. E. O'CALLAGHAN. Manchester, 16th November, 1912.

THE REAL WARFARE.

WHERE GERMANY LEADS THE WAY.

INTERESTING SPEECH AT TECHNICAL

INSTITUTE PRIZE DISTRIBUTION.

"The true warfare is not fights on sea or land, but in the sphere of knowledge," declared Mr. J. 11. Reynolds, M.c. (late Director of Education for Manchester and Principal of the Municipal t:chool of Technology, Manchester) in a highly interesting speech at the Parr Ball on Wednesday evening. .1 he occasion was the annual distribution of prizes and certificates to students of the School of Art, Technical Institute and Commercial Institute. It was eminently fitting that in a manufacturing town like \\ arrington, Mr. Reynolds should devote the greater part of his address to emphasising the paramount importance of education in retaining our commercial supremacy. He also made much of the application of scientific methods to industry. Before the meeting began, Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., gave an organ recital which was much appreciated. It is, however, a matter for regret that no steps have yet been taken to obtain an instrument worthy of our principal public hall, the present one falling woefully short of that standard. During the evening Miss Sara Silcock very sweetly and artistically rendered " The Valley of Laughter " and "0 Flower of all the World." Councillor H Roberts (chairman of the Education Committee) presided over a large gathering of studeuts and friends. Amongst ()tire's on tee platform were : Mrs Roberts, AW Jackson Holmes, the Rev C inon Stevenson, Alderman Smethurst, Councillors 'lades and Hensuall, Mr CE Parker, Mr John Upson, Mr Moore Murray (director), Mr R B Roberts (assistant director) and members of the teaching stalls. WHY GERMANY HAS ADVANCED. THE REAL WARFARE.

Mr REYNOLDS began his address by describing War- rington as a town of no small repute—au ancient town which went back for its origin to Roman and Saxon times and which had played its part in history in the Norman and Commonwealth period's. Having referred to the important geographical position of the town and to its variety of industries which enabled it to meet fluctations and adversity with more confidence than communities which depended upon one trade,t he speaker said the aims of the borough bore the motto "It is God who gives tne increase.''At ter OA was well to remember that [leaven helped those who helped themselves and he hoped every Warringtonian who looked upon that motto would realize that unless there were some ploughing and seed-sowing, no increase could possibly come or deserve to come. (Applause.) Warrington had an enterprising Corporation. The people owned all sorts of municipal undertakings and he always thought it was well when one could say of a town that all that made for the comforts and amenties of life on a large scale was held in the firm hands of the people them- selves. (Applause.) There were few questions of more importance than those which related to provision for continued education. They had in Warrington, so far as evening continuation classes were concerned, some 1,300 students. While that was an enormous improvement on what had gone on in the past, it was far from satisfying those who were seriously concerned with measures for securing the con- tinued education of the children on leaving school. When one called to mind that there were in Warring- ton, between the ages of 14 and 21, more than 9,000 young people of bosh sexes, it showed what an enormous labour lay before the Education Committee in trying to account for the difference between 1,300 and 9,000. They needed to reduce it by some thousands and Warrington would then be a very different place in many ways from what it was now. He was concerned about the question of continued education not so much from the point of view of increasing the amount of information that students could receive after leaving school, but because care should be taken for the improvement of character and for the better training of students in the work • in which they were engaged, with the object of securing the fullest efficiency. GERMAN-Y WAS MUCH TO THE FRONT NOWADAYS.

She deserved to be, in many ways, and it was our business to look at tier calmly and dispassionately. One hundred and six years ago this very year,alter the battle of Jena, and when Prussia lay prostrated before the victorious arms of France led by her imperial master Napoleon, when the French entered lierlin the then King of Prussia used the words : " Germans must regain by intellectual power what she has lost in material power. For this purpose, it is my desire that everything possible should be done to extend and perfect the education of the people" Led by men like Humboldt, they founded the University of Berlin which ' to-day had probably more students than any other I University in Europe, which was surrounded by a galaxy• of some of the most learned men in the world, the the fruits of whose knowledge and research had gone a long way to place Germany in the position she enjoyed to-day. She was now a United empire, consisting of many States, each with its own Government but animated by 000 policy and by a single desire to further the !Anse of education. Trio rulers and leading minds of Germany were quite satisfied that the position she held to-day was

NOT VIZ RESULT 01 VICTORIOUS ARMIES.

but of the wise provision made generations ago for securing the right education of the people in all depart- ments of human knowledge and enterprise. He remem- bered being in Paris on the occasion of the centennial exposition in 1900 and in one department he saw a splendid collection of scientific instruments of precision, got together by 700 German manufacturers. In the preface to the catalogue it was stated: " the position that Germany occupies to-day as manufacturers of glasslor use in scientific methods of research and as makers of various fine instruments of precision, the fact that we no longer depend upon France or England, but find a market for our goods in both countries, is due to the close association of the manufacturers with the men of science in the Universi- ties." (Applause.) We wanted to bring that about in England to-day. We had seldom believed in it ; we had looked upon the professor and the scientific man in the University as more or less of a pedant, unacquainted with practical affairs and, therefore, not of much use to the practical, industrial manufacturer. That day was passing, but he was afraid we bad left it too long. We had been so satisfied that whatever we cared to make was sure to find a market that we had not looked far enough into the future. To speak of the chemical indu3try was a commonplace, but ono thing was certain—that so far as the fine chemical industry was concerned, the beginnings of which were made in England, it had

GONE FROM THIS COUNTRY FOR EVER and what had happened in that relation would happen in other great industries if we were'not wide awake to the conditions under which industry and commerce were carried on. The German Technical High School was a day iastitution attended by students who had completed up to their eighteenth year, the course of an ordinary school educa- tion and who went to the Technical High School for three or four years to receive a specialised course of training in such industries as they intended to enter. Contrast that with the oonditions in England. Into the technical schools—into the Universities, for that matter—the students entered usually at 16 or 17 years of age, with nothing like the previous training that a German Student enjoyed. He often felt that we in England had set our professors to deal with students after the fashion of a man who used the fine-edged razor to chop wood. In Germany they got students properly prepared for their work. No wonder they turned out what Bismarck once termed an educated proletariat, for which they had little occupation, so he thought. But of one thing he (the speaker) was certain—that the Germans would never have succeeded in their endeavour to raise the chemical industry to the point it had reached, had they not had at their command an enormous supply of well- trained young men. RECORD BREAKING AT BBOOKLANDS.

The Invincible Talbot celebrated the conclusion of the Olympla Show on Saturday by such an attack upon the records at Brooklands as resulted in their unprecedented slaughter. The car, which was a 25 h p., with the ordinary standard engine catalogued and supplied by Messrs. Clement Talbot, Ltd., first of all put up the terrific speed of 113 28 miles per hour. The flying kilometer was done at 112 81, the mile at 1111.3 miles per hour, while her fastest lap (about three miles) was completed at 10043 miles per hour— a record lap at Brooklauds. So fast was the car travelling that when, at the finish, the driver cut off his power, she ran with her own velocity a distance of nearly two miles. The car was driven by Mr. Percy Lambert who has been so successful as a (Myer of Pearley 111 and other famous cats.

The character of the Talbot performance can be gathered from the fact that the previous record for 4in. engines was beaten by no less than 26 m ph., and with the exception of the 200 h.p, Benz, the 25 h p. falnot ott Stturday secured the record of being the fastest car that has ever travelled on the Brooklands track. At the conclusion of the record breaking, Major Lloyd officially inspected the engine and certified it to be slightly smaller than the official requirements. A little item of inlet-frit to every motorist was that at the conclusion of the trial the Palmer tyres with which the car was fitted showed not a mark—testitnony to the wonderful springing of the Talbot, 811,1 the value of the NA= shock absorbets. The car was fitted with the Stewart-Talbot catburettar. ed 13,,seh It is a futther Dotewerthy instance of the invincibility of the Invincible Talbot that for the last two yrars in the Welsh Automobile Club Hill-climb Talbots have scored Ist, 2nd and 3rd places.

THAT IS WHERS THE TRUE WARFARE LIES —not in lights on sea or hind —those do not make it nation great or secure its progress—out in fights in that sphere of knowledge. Huxley never said a truer thing than that the real and most terrible warfare wss the industrial wartara and the nation which is prepared to meet it, whick secur.-s the neces-ary weapons for the service of its people, will win in the long ruin. (Applause.) The German huts set himself to do it, and small nations like Switzerland, with a population not greater than that of Lanca- shire, are spending untold sums of money with the sure object.' Continuing, Mr Reynolds urged upon the people of Warrington that they should do all in their power to give the advantages of education to all the children of the town. He did not say that all the children were worthy of the best efforts of the citizens because we were so varied in gifts. As a friend once said to him, there were 50 per cent, of folk of a very ordinary character, who could just do the labouring work, if they liked, 36 or 40 per cent with some power of iniative and 5 or 10 per cent. geniuses. We had to do what Huxley suggested—drop a grappling iron in the midst of the children of a city and endeavour to pick out those who were worthy of all the money, time and labour that could be expended upon them. They might find such a one in the poorest cottage in Warrington. They never knew where talent lay. It was not necessarily, in the rich home with the best surroundings and advantages. Assuming that ten children for every 1,000 inhabitants of Warrington were well worth looking after, Warrington ought to have 700 children to whom those special advantages should be extended. The town was a long way from that position. In a German town of the size of Warrington, instead of there being two secondary schools, there would be at least four or five. We had not risen to that conception ; we were in such a precious hnrry to get the children to work and to take them away from school advantages. Mr. Reynolds instanced the great outcry at the abolition of child labour in the Lancashire factories. It was said at the time that the Lancashire cotton trade would be doomed, but it was not doomed and Oldham was not a deserted or ruined place. We had now got beyond that narrow, short-sighted view and were on the way, he hoped, to a much more generous consideration of the claims of the child. We did not want to think that England, sooner or later, would follow the fate of Persia, Rome or Egypt, but we should surely be near to that disaster unless we took care in every possible way to secure the best education—and by that he meant not simply the educa- tion of " getting on," but the entire and complete education of the man or woman so that they could live the life of a citizen worthy of such a kingdom and Empire as ours. Urging the importance of teaching the beantieS and power of English language and literature, Mr ltu ynolds said that once they got a child to appreciate a good book they could leave him very much to take care of himself, he would look after his own education. The teachers should be entrusted with the deliberate selec- tion of children in the elementary schools of whom they could say at twelve years of age: "This child is worth four or five years of sedulous training. He will do credit to the town of Warring- ton and will be of great service to the nation, in whatever sphere he may be called upon to serve." They would then get in the secondary schools not those who went for a polish or finish who could say " I went a year to the Boteler Grammar School or the Municipal Secondary School." They did not want that kind of child at all. They wanted the child who could pass through the influences of the Secondary School and remain there until he was sixteen or seventeen years of age. He hoped the day would come when we would do as in Germany,and when a boy had thoroughly satisfied the conditions of Secondary School training he Noonld pass without further examination to the higher spheres of learning in the University. (Applause). We should in that way put the elementary schoois upon a proper footing as the vestibule to Uni versities,which would then get a better supply of students than was now the case. Mr Reynolds pleaded for the close co-operation of the the employes in regard to the attendance of the workpeople at evening classes. A great deal was being said about

COMPULSION. Personally, he believed that if we were to have com- pulsion, it should be placed upon the right shoulders. It should not he placed upon the parents of our elemen- tary school children, who bad difnculties enough to con- tend with. A boy of fourteen—or a girl either— was not, as things went, particularly easy to manage. Ile (the speaker) was the enemy of half-time before the age of fourteen —it was the most evil thing that ever came upon us—but he was the friend of half-time between the ages of fourteen and seventeen. fie thought the employers might be content with some- thing like 30 Of?. 35 nouns A WEEK from every employee between the ages of fourteen and seventeen—(applause)--and that they should only per- mit the employment of boys or girls in their service, who were using the remaining hours of the week to the entire satisfaction of the employer and of the Education Committee, so far as their continued education was concerned. That was the only way in which they had the right to expect compulsion to operate successfully. There never was a time so great with momentous events as the present day. They might read in history all about the Wars of the Roses, the Civil War and other events, but they paled in comparison with the problems that beset the most thinking minds of England to-day. They had to be prepared to face these difficult questions and there was no other way of doing it except by taking advantage of the education which was offered by the Education Com- mittee of their town. Success would never be achieved except by sedulous, persevering, constant effort. THE AGE OF LEAVING SCHOOL. YOUR CHILD'S LUNGS.

Peps Protect and Strengthen the Chest.

Every mother should keep a box of silver-jacketed Peps in the house at this time of the year when coughs, colds and sore throat are particularly rife amongst children. If these ailments are not checked at the beginning with Peps, there is danger that serious throat sod chest weakness in later life may be the unhappy consequence. The chest-strengthening properties that make the pine forests of Switzerland famous are concentrated in Peps. When a Peps tablet dissolves in the mouth these properties are released in the form of fumes that are breathed down the throat straight into the lungs, where liquid mixtures cannot go. By going direct to the seat of the trouble, Pens quickly stop a cough and wheeziness, keep the little ones' throats clear of phlegm, soothe and strengthen the delicate membrane that lines the air passages from throat to lungs, and prevent infection by disease germs. This Peps treatment is not only the natural treat- ment ftr coughs and colds, but Peps are so entirely free from dangerous drugs that they may be given to the most delicate child with perfect safety. Don't be content with the thought that when the warm weather comes again your child will lose his cough. That may be true, but mischief will be done to the lungs. Directly hoarseness and huskiness tell of trouble in the throat, give Peps to the children freely and they will be kept free from coughs, elide, croup, whooping cough, infantile bronchitis, and all such chest and throat troubles. Peps will help and encourage the children to breathe deeply by filling the lungs and chest with pure and comforting pine-forest air. Thus, in Nature's own way, Peps make the children deep-chested instead of weak-chested. See that the name Peps is on the box you buy from your chemist. Avoid worth- less substitutes.

LOCAL PARLIAMENT. INSURANCE ACT CARRIED WITH SOCIALIST AMENDMENT.

With the aid of the Socialist votes, the Conservative Government in the Parliamentary Debating Society passed its National Insurance Act on Thursday evening. In order to enlist the support of the section whose advanced views they generally deride, the Government agreed to incorporate in the original measure the amendment carried by the Socialists last week, making certain exceptions with regard to contributions and benefits in favour of the poorer classes of contributors. The maternity benefit is also to be granted to all insured mothers. The Liberals did not challenge a division, the Leader of the Opposition (Mr J Jarman) remarking: "The coalition is too strong for us," while another " voice" interjected" Poor despised Socialists ! " MrR G OWEN (L., Chelmsford) commended the flat rate as laid down in Mr Lloyd George's measure on the ground that it accepted the principle that the employer was interest( d in the health of the worker.Fle asked how the scale of contributicns proposed in the present Bill would operate in the case of workers whose wages repeatedly fluctuated? 1 Mr E ENGLAND (1., Warrington) said Mr Lloyd George stood before tne British public as the most unpopular politician of the day. (Tony " Hear, hears" and Opposition cries of "Question "). Why ? Because he had launched a life boat, known as the National Insurance Act, which was badly equipped and manned. He ventured to predict, however, that in the course of a few months the Chancellor would be the most popular statesman of the hour. Whatever its imperfections, the Act would be the means of saving many thousands of lives. (The LEADER OF TILE OPPODTION : "In spite of being badly manned and equipped.") The Speaker predicted that next year there would be great rejoicings in many homes because the maternity benefit would have come into force, and that the young hopeful would invari- ably be christened "David," What flame would be chosen for the girls he did not know. (A VOICE I "Georgina " anct much laughter.) Whatever failure might have attached to any of Mr. Lloyd George's financial proposals, he was undoubtedly one of the greatest humanitarian statesmen that this country had ever produced, and his memoiy would be cherished for many generations.

Tl;e-brEAKER : I must ask the hon. member to cUscuss the Bill.

Mr ENGLAND expressed surprise at the attitude of the Opposition towards the present measure. They appeared to be unable to rid themselves of the fatal spirit expressed in Biblical times in the phrase " Can any good thing come out of Nazareth ?" They looked with suspicion upon anything which emlnated from the Conservative side. (The LEADER OF TILE OPPOSITION : "it is experience.") The Socialists had considerably strengthened the measure by carrying the clause extend- ing the niNternity benefits to all insured mothers. He considered that if it were only for the maternity benefit, Mr Lloyd George's Act was worthy of being placed on the Statute book. _

I Mr P G'ButeN (L., St Pancras) said the last speaber was one of the most delightful specimens of contradic- I Oen that he bad had the pleasure of meeting. (Laughter.) He had spoken very approvingly of the Imperial Act, which the Member for Westheughton (Mr GI F Clarhe) had characterised as one of the most gigantic frauds and swindles ever perpetrated on the I people of this country. Oa the other baud he gave his support to the measure which was new brought forward to repeal Mr Lloyd George's Act. If the latter Act was so bad as the Tories made out, he could only , stigmatise the House of Lords as alders and abetters in the swindle. (Laughter.) Where was the sacrifice of the great " Die Hards " who professed to watch the interests of the people but yet dill not take advautage of theirlpoweis to delay the Bill ? Had it been a Bill to rncroach upon the privileges or monopolies of a class, they would undoubtedly have so eroaculated and coloured it that its own parent would not have kuoen it. (Laughter). The speaker spoke of the Lecessity of raising the wages of the labouring class to euable them better to meet the insurance demands. At the seine time, there were labourers in this twin who had been earning lBs. per week and had had 2d. deducted, fur medical attendance. Yet no fuss had been mine about it and if they considered the bern.fi s which the extra 2d. under Mr Lloyd George's Act would procure, there eras no great injustice to labour. The speaker cornmeuden the principle that everybody who received the benefits of insurance should pay tor them. They would then get them as a right and not as charity. (Applause). The PRESIDENT Or THE BOARD OF TRADE (Mr. P. Davenport) said the Opposition had deliberately avoided the principles of the Bill and had contented themselves with trying to pick holes in very unimportant details. It was typical of the attitude of the Liberals in the Imperial Parliament—they did not view legislation from the standpoint of the public good, but tried to regulate their conduct so that their party would benefit, irrespective of the national results. The present Act was a great ads ante, in that thcse afflicted with sickness at the present time would come into full benefit. Under Mr Lloyd George's Act, those unfortunate individuals were left outside its scope as far as money benefits were concerned. The graduated scale of contributions was based on a sound principle and would help the poorer classes of workers. Mr T HOWARD (S., W. Bradford) said a mrasure emanating from any capitalistic party was viewed with suspicion by any person of advanced opinion and the present Bill was no exception. It was a general endorse- ment of the fundamental principles embodied in Mr Lloyd George's Act. In some slight degree it modified the contributions and extended the benefits. If the Bill was intended to better the conditions of the working classes or of those on the poverty line, he was afraid the Government were labouring undera delusion. They had not taken into consideration the economic conditions under which the working classes lived. The workingman who was unemployed had sufficient leeway to make up in rent and other debts to justify his excustil from arrears in insurance contributions. What was the use of all the glorious sanatoria, if alter having been treated there the patient was to be sent hank to the deplorable slum dwellings where many of them contracted the disease? „ .

Mr L Warburton (C., W Bristol) claimed that the Bill would remove the absurdities and inequalities of IMr Lloyd George's Act. Every endeavour had been made to meet the needs of those classes who most required insurance. According to the report of the General Federation of Trade Unions the cost of •pro- viding and administering the benefits of the present Act would exceed the cost of the benefits themselves and it was further alleged that the interests of the unorganised workers had been subordinated to the fads of officials. Ile contended that the working of the scheme should be left entirely to the friendly societies as more personal interest would then be taken in it. Councillor HAWTMORN (L.' Montrose), said it would have come with better grace from the Conservatives if they had waited until Mr Lloyd George's Act was in full operation before proceeding to nullify it." (A SOCIALIST : "ft would have been too late then.") No opportunity had been given to discover the detects of the measure. The Tory Bill would have been better entitled "An Employers' Relief Bill." Why should they go scot free? If the contributory principle was to be observed, it was only fair that both sides should pay their share. If Germany was the pattern country which tariff reformers represented it to be, why did the Government not follow the example of Germany in compelling the employer to con- tribute to health insurance? The speaker emphasised the fact that the Bill did not contain the provisions which were to be found in Mr Lloyd George's Act for striking at the slum evil by compell- ing the owners of insanitary houses and workshops to pay for the eccessive sickness attributable to them. The Bid would also bring about an inquisition as to the wages of insured persons, in order to carry out the sliding scale. The wages sheet was not always a true criterion of what a man earned (Socialist applause, and a Voice : "it is of what he gets.") Mr Lloyd George's Act marshalled the entire community to the I help of the worker who became incapacitated through physical breakdown. The Tory Bill was only a partial measure and a very weak imitation of the original. The PRIME MINISTER (Mr E A White) said the Government considered the Bill,even with the Socialist amendment, to be an improvement on Mr. Lloyd George's Act and they were therefore willing to incor- porate the amendment. He contended that tha flat rate in Mr Lloyd George's Act was unfair in putting all workers on a uniform footing, irrespective of age, health or class of occupation. It was all very well to say it was their duty to provide for their fellow-men, but why should that duty be left only to the workers ? He contended that any irregularities should be met by the nation at large, Wages depended on the profits the employer could make and the strength of the men's unions. It wee quite clear that the men, with their unions, would have as large wages as they could get and I speaking generally the employers would pay as small wages as they could. That was the principle and both employers and workmen recognised that there was a point beyond abich neither could go. It logically followed that any impost levied on an industry, whether charged on the employer or the worker, inevitably felt on the worker, and he contended that the insurance levy, although in form it might be partly collected from the employer, would in nearly all cases fall on the worker. The speaker contended that Mr Lloyd George's Act was deficent in that it made no satisfactory provision for the Casual workers. Mr WATSON (S., E Birmingham) said the Bill as amended was a vast improvement on Mr Lloyd George's Act. He contended, however, that to be entirely satis- factory an insurance scheme must provide the worlse with the full amount of his wages during sickness. Admirals, judges and members of Parliament received that consideration and why not the Workingmen? The great fault of Mr Lloyd George's Act was that the employer deducted the contribution from the wages and stamped the card which then became a man's passport, exactly as they found in those Continental nations where conscription was in force. Probably the idea of the card system was to give the conscription idea a footing in England. (Liberal laughter and a VOICE: "Lloyd George as a conscript I") The Bill as amended, was then passed twithout a division.

USINESS HOURS 9 to 8 DAIL'

A HOUSING BILL. Next Thursday evening the President of the Local Government Board (sir Norman Nield) will introduce a Bill to provide for the better application and enforce" taAnt of the Housing of the Working Classes Acts, 1890-1909, and to amend the Small Dwellings Requisi- tion Act, 1899. Its provisions include : The Local Go, ernment Board may make an order authorising a local authority to aquire an estate or land exceeding ten acres for the purpose of Part 111 of the 1890 act. A local authority, with the consent of the Loma Government Board may release any bye-laws with respect to streets and buildings. A local authority should have power to PAY 4 compensation to the owner of property demolished al unfit for habitation,where the owner agrees no building shall be erected upon the cleared site except with the consent of the local authority. There shall be annually paid out of the Consolidated Fund a sum (to be called the housing grant) of £500,000, to be distributed as the Local Government Board may determine among local authorities.

RUNCORN UNION.—SUPERINTENDENT AND MATRON. The Guardians of the above Union invite applications for the appointment of Superintendent and Matron of their Children's Home, situate at Dutton near Warrington. Candidates must be husband and wife, the husband must have a knowledge of a trade and Gardening, and the wife should have a knowledge of Cookery and Laundry work. The husband's age must not be less than 30 years and not more than 45 years. Salary.—Superintendent, £4O ' • Matron, £25 ; with apartments, rations, kc., in the Home. The appointment will be subject to the provisions of the Poor Law Officers Superannuation Act, 1896. and the Superintendent will be required to give a guarantee in an approved Guarantee Society for the sum of £lOO. Applications in the handwriting of the candidates, stating age, present and previous occupation, accom- panied by three recent testinomials, and endorsed " Appointment of Superintendent and Matron," mutt be sent so as to reach the undersigned not later than 10 o'clock a.m., on Monday, the 2nd December, 1912. Application forms will not be issued. Canvassing of the Guardians, either directly or indirectly, will be deemed a disqualification. Selected candidates will receive notice to attend when required, and third-class railway fares will be allowed. The persons appointed must be prepared to enter Upon their duties on a date in January nest. GEO. F. ASHTON, Clerk to the Guardians. Union Offices, 71, High St., Runeorn, 20th November, 1912.

Last week we showed by diagram how, since the introduction of the last Gerroan Tariff in 1906,a tariff designed to increase the proportion oflexports of manufactured goods, I the effect had been the direct opposite, this proportion having dropped. This week 011„,1. diagram illustratesithe actual exports of Cotton Manufactures, excluding yarns, of GerMa,s and the United Kingdom. It will be seen that the effect of the German Tariff of has been actually to reduce the exports of cotton manufactures, while our own exports 0' the same goods have been advancing by the proverbial leaps and bounds. It is frequent' brought forward by Tariff Reformers that Germany is increasing her exports of coal goods to the countries which surround her frontiers in Europe. This may be so, and 1, is to be expected, but it must be at the expense of the trade with other countries, as h.e' total export of cotton goods is practically stationary. Surely the total export is the Piil? cipal point to consider and compare, and our diagram shows how, in this, Free Trade 19 once more triumphant.
